**Reform UK**, also known as the **Reform Party** or simply **Reform**, is a [right-wing populist](8354382.md) and [far-right political party in the United Kingdom](3884621.md). It has eight [members of Parliament](3776360.md) in the [House of Commons](13828.md), two members of the [London Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/London_Assembly), thirty-three [members of the Senedd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Member_of_the_Senedd), seventeen [members of the Scottish Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Member_of_the_Scottish_Parliament), two [police and crime commissioners](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Police_and_crime_commissioner), and controls twenty-six local councils. It is to the right of the [Conservative Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_Party_\(UK\)). [Nigel Farage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nigel_Farage) has been [leader of Reform UK](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leader_of_Reform_UK) since June 2024.

The party was co-founded by Farage and [Catherine Blaiklock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catherine_Blaiklock) in 2018 as the **Brexit Party**, advocating a [no-deal Brexit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/No-deal_Brexit); it won the most seats at the [2019 European Parliament election in the UK](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_European_Parliament_election_in_the_UK). The [UK withdrew](41688778.md) from the [European Union](9317.md) in January 2020, and later in the same year the [COVID-19 pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_the_United_Kingdom) began in the UK. The Conservative government imposed [a series of national lockdowns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_lockdown_in_the_United_Kingdom) and Farage focused on anti-lockdown campaigning. The party was renamed Reform UK in January 2021. Farage stepped down as leader in March 2021 and was succeeded by [Richard Tice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Tice).

Classified as part of the [radical right](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radical_right_\(Europe\)), since 2022, the party has campaigned on a broader platform, pledging to reduce [taxation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taxation_in_the_United_Kingdom), limit [immigration](1247786.md), oppose [net-zero emissions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Net-zero_emissions) policies and substantially reduce public spending. In March 2024, [Lee Anderson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lee_Anderson), who was elected in 2019 as a Conservative MP, [defected to Reform UK](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Conservative_Party_defections_to_Reform_UK), becoming its first MP. In June 2024, Farage resumed the leadership. The party won five seats at the [2024 general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_United_Kingdom_general_election), the first time it had elected MPs.

## History

### Brexit Party

The incorporation of the Brexit Party in November 2018[^cite_note-Company-10] was formally announced on 20 January 2019 by the former [UK Independence Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UK_Independence_Party) (UKIP) economics spokesperson[^cite_note-ukip1-11] [Catherine Blaiklock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catherine_Blaiklock), who served as the Brexit Party's initial leader.[^cite_note-independent-12] On 5 February 2019, it was registered with the [Electoral Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electoral_Commission_\(United_Kingdom\)) to run candidates in English, Scottish, Welsh and European Union elections.[^cite_note-electoralcommission.org.uk-13]

On the day of the announcement, [Nigel Farage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nigel_Farage), who had been an independent [member of the European Parliament](258909.md) (MEP) since his departure from UKIP in early December 2018, said that the party was Blaiklock's idea but that she had acted with his full support.[^cite_note-independent-12] On 8 February 2019, Farage stated he would stand as a candidate for the party in any potential [future European Parliament elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_European_Parliament_election_in_the_United_Kingdom) contested in the United Kingdom.[^cite_note-\:9-14][^cite_note-\:10-15] The MEPs [Steven Woolfe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steven_Woolfe) and [Nathan Gill](42863259.md), also formerly of UKIP, stated that they would also stand for the party.[^cite_note-Newsnight-16][^cite_note-\:11-17]

The party's lead aim was for the United Kingdom to leave the EU, and then for Britain to trade internationally on [World Trade Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Trade_Organization) terms.[^cite_note-bbc.com-18] In April 2019, Farage said that there was "no difference between the Brexit party and UKIP in terms of policy, \[but] in terms of personnel, there's a vast difference", criticising UKIP's connections to the far right. He also said that the party aimed to attract support from "across the board", including former UKIP voters and Conservative and [Labour](19279158.md) voters who had supported Brexit.[^cite_note-guardian-19] Later in the month he said that the party would not publish a manifesto until after the European elections had taken place,[^cite_note-20] saying that the party would have a policy platform instead of a manifesto.[^cite_note-21]

In May 2019, Farage described his admiration for how fellow [Europe of Freedom and Direct Democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Europe_of_Freedom_and_Direct_Democracy) members, Italy's [Five Star Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Five_Star_Movement), had managed to grow from a protest group into the country's largest political party in both houses of the [Italian Parliament](1417859.md). He saw the Brexit Party doing the same kind of thing and "running a company, not a political party, hence our model of registered supporters" and building a base using an online platform.[^cite_note-22]

On 11 November 2019, the last day for candidates to register, Farage declared that the Brexit Party would not field candidates in the 317 seats in which there was an incumbent Conservative MP. This was done with the support of most of the Brexit Party candidates, so as not to split the anti-EU vote.[^cite_note-\:14-23]

On 22 November 2019, the Brexit Party set out its proposals for the [2019 general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_United_Kingdom_general_election). They covered a wide range of policy areas including taxation, reforming politics, immigration and the environment.[^cite_note-BP-24][^cite_note-CP-25] The party received two percent of the vote in the election, with none of its 273 candidates winning a seat.[^cite_note-killed-26]

### Transition into Reform UK



Deputy leader and former party leader, [Richard Tice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Tice)

Before the general election on 8 December 2019, Farage announced that, following Brexit, the party would change its name to the "Reform Party", and campaign for changes in the electoral system and structure of the House of Commons.[^cite_note-bbc-reformparty-27][^cite_note-registration1-28] Farage has stated that he admired the [Reform Party of Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reform_Party_of_Canada) in the 1990s, which both influenced his politics and the naming of Reform UK; further, [Preston Manning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Preston_Manning), the historic leader of the Reform Party of Canada, attended a Reform UK event alongside Farage in [Birmingham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birmingham) on 5 September 2025.[^cite_note-Tasker-29]

In November 2020, Farage and Tice announced that they had applied to the Electoral Commission to rename the Brexit Party to 'Reform UK'. They said that the party would campaign on a platform that was opposed to further [COVID-19 lockdowns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_lockdowns) and that it would seek to reform aspects of the [British government](25318118.md), including the [BBC](19344654.md) and the [House of Lords](13658.md).[^cite_note-AL-30][^cite_note-FL-31] The party also gave its support to the [Great Barrington Declaration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Barrington_Declaration).[^cite_note-\:15-32] On 4 January 2021, the party's name change to Reform UK was approved by the [Electoral Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electoral_Commission_\(United_Kingdom\)).[^cite_note-registration1-28]

In 2021, Reform UK gained representation in the [Scottish Parliament](61188.md) when [Michelle Ballantyne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michelle_Ballantyne), then an [independent](2172378.md) and formerly a Conservative [member of the Scottish Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Member_of_the_Scottish_Parliament) (MSP), joined the party and was named Reform UK's leader in Holyrood.[^cite_note-\:16-33] She lost her and the party's only seat in Scotland in the [2021 Scottish Parliament election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Scottish_Parliament_election),[^cite_note-National_Feb_2022-34][^cite_note-Herald_Feb_2022-35] and resigned as the party's leader in Scotland in February 2022.[^cite_note-National_Feb_2022-34][^cite_note-Herald_Feb_2022-35]

Farage stepped down as leader in March 2021, being replaced by the party chairman, Tice.[^cite_note-36] The former [North West England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_West_England_\(European_Parliament_constituency\)) MEP [David Bull](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Bull_\(politician\)) was appointed as deputy leader of the party on 11 March 2021.[^cite_note-37][^cite_note-\:8-38] On 26 March 2021, it was announced that the former Brexit Party MEP [Nathan Gill](42863259.md) had become the Leader of Reform UK Wales.[^cite_note-39] In 2021, Reform UK announced its intention to field a full slate of candidates in the [Senedd](238480.md), [Scottish Parliament](61188.md) and [London Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/London_Assembly) elections with Tice standing for election in the latter.[^cite_note-40][^cite_note-41][^cite_note-42] The party failed to win any seats above local level in the 2021 elections in May, and lost their deposit in the [Hartlepool by-election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Hartlepool_by-election).[^cite_note-43]

At the [2021 Senedd election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Senedd_election), the party fielded candidates in every constituency and on the regional lists; it picked up 1.6% of the constituency vote (7th place) and 1.1% of the regional list votes (8th place).[^cite_note-44] At the [2021 Scottish Parliament election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Scottish_Parliament_election) no constituency candidates were fielded and the party received only 5,793 list votes across the whole country.[^cite_note-45] At the [London Assembly election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_London_Assembly_election) none of their constituency candidates were elected and the party finished tenth on the London-wide list with 25,009 votes.[^cite_note-\:8-38][^cite_note-46]



Party leader [Nigel Farage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nigel_Farage)

In October 2022, Reform UK and the [Social Democratic Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_Democratic_Party_\(UK,_1990%E2%80%93present\)) (SDP) announced an [electoral pact](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electoral_pact).[^cite_note-47][^cite_note-48] Tice declared Reform's intention to stand in 630 constituencies across England, Scotland and Wales with "no ifs, no buts".[^cite_note-bbc-rules-out-49] In December 2022 David White, a Conservative member of [Barnsley Metropolitan Borough Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barnsley_Metropolitan_Borough_Council), and Richard Langridge, a Conservative member of [West Oxfordshire District Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Oxfordshire), both [defected to Reform UK](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Conservative_Party_defections_to_Reform_UK) to stand as [prospective parliamentary candidates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prospective_parliamentary_candidate) for the party.[^cite_note-50][^cite_note-51]

The media gave renewed attention to Reform UK in December 2022 during the [cost-of-living crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021%E2%80%93present_United_Kingdom_cost-of-living_crisis) after Farage announced that it would stand a full slate of candidates at the next general election.[^cite_note-52][^cite_note-53][^cite_note-54] Tice remained leader of the party. After some opinion polls indicated a modest increase in support for Reform UK, *[The Daily Telegraph](23797577.md)* described the party as a "threat on the Right" to the Conservative government of Prime Minister [Rishi Sunak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rishi_Sunak).[^cite_note-55]

### 2024 general election

On 7 and 8 October 2023, Reform UK held its party conference in London with 1,100 attendees.[^cite_note-56] On 20 October 2023 Tice confirmed that Reform UK would stand in Conservative seats at the [2024 general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_United_Kingdom_general_election),[^cite_note-The_Daily_Telegraph,_20_October_2023-57] and by January 2024 the party was polling around 10% of the popular vote. It was suggested that Reform UK would play the role of [spoiler party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spoiler_effect) for the Conservatives, since it attracted former Conservative voters. *[The Guardian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Guardian)* speculated that votes for the party could lead to more than 30 additional seat losses for the Conservative Party.[^cite_note-58]

Logos of the Reform UK–TUV alliance





In Northern Ireland, in March 2024, the party formed an [electoral pact](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reform_UK%E2%80%93TUV_alliance) with the [Traditional Unionist Voice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Traditional_Unionist_Voice) (TUV), in which the two parties would stand mutually agreed candidates there.[^cite_note-TUV-59] In May 2024 [Alex Wilson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alex_Wilson_\(British_politician\)) became Reform's first London Assembly member, elected via the London-wide voting system.[^cite_note-London_Elects_2024-60] On 3 June 2024 Farage replaced Tice as leader of the party.[^cite_note-guardian-u-turn-61] It gained five MPs in England in the July 2024 general election,[^cite_note-meet-62] and its Northern Irish affiliate TUV gaining one seat with [Jim Allister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jim_Allister).[^cite_note-63]

In March 2024, [Simon Danczuk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simon_Danczuk), the former [Labour](19279158.md) MP for [Rochdale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rochdale_\(constituency\)), joined Reform and stood as their parliamentary candidate in the [2024 Rochdale by-election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Rochdale_by-election).[^cite_note-64] Danczuk received 6.3% of the vote, thus saving his deposit, but was not elected. The seat was won by [George Galloway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Galloway).[^cite_note-65]

Analysis in March 2024 by [Matt Goodwin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matt_Goodwin) for the [Legatum Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legatum_Institute) showed that support for Reform, like UKIP and the Brexit Party before it, was strongest among older voters and those who voted Leave, and relatively even across social classes. By [NRS social grades](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NRS_social_grade), 36% of likely Reform voters were in AB, 22% in C1, 23% in C2 and 19% in DE.[^cite_note-66]

In May 2024, Farage declared that Reform is becoming a "brand new [Conservative](6675.md) movement", while criticising Labour and Conservative immigration policies as being indistinguishable from each other.[^cite_note-\:7-67]



Zia Yusuf addressing a Reform UK pre-election rally at the [NEC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Exhibition_Centre) near [Birmingham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birmingham) on 30 June 2024

Following the election, on 11 July, the businessman [Zia Yusuf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zia_Yusuf) replaced Tice as chairman of the party, with Tice, now an MP, replacing [Ben Habib](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ben_Habib) as deputy leader.[^cite_note-68][^cite_note-YahooTel-69][^cite_note-70] The party plans to stand at the [2026 Scottish Parliament election](65934215.md),[^cite_note-71] and expected to win significantly in the [2026 Senedd election](68245236.md) in Wales, under the [new more proportional system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Senedd_Cymru_\(Members_and_Elections\)_Act_2024).[^cite_note-72] In September 2024 Farage said that he would be surrendering all of his shares in Reform UK. This would mean members would have more control over the party, such as being able to vote on a constitution and motions, and could remove Farage as leader if over 50% of members wrote to Yusuf.[^cite_note-members-73] In October 2024 Farage called for Conservative councillors to join Reform UK and said "a huge number of them genuinely agree with us and what we stand for".[^cite_note-Tory_councillors-74]

In November 2024, it was reported that senior members of the party were divided about supporters of political activist [Tommy Robinson](32537879.md), with two of the party's parliamentary candidates expressing sympathy for some of the supporters of Robinson who took part in August's anti-immigration protests, in the face of objections from Tice and Farage.[^cite_note-75][^cite_note-76] For the free vote on the [assisted suicide bill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terminally_Ill_Adults_\(End_of_Life\)_Bill), Tice, [Lee Anderson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lee_Anderson) and [Rupert Lowe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rupert_Lowe) supported the bill in its second reading, whilst Farage and [James McMurdock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_McMurdock) opposed it.[^cite_note-77]

In November and December 2024, several high-profile Conservatives quit that party and joined Reform. These included the former MP [Andrea Jenkyns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrea_Jenkyns),[^cite_note-78] former MP [Lucy Allan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lucy_Allan_\(politician\)), [Tim Montgomerie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tim_Montgomerie) (founder of [ConservativeHome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ConservativeHome) and adviser to [Boris Johnson](19065069.md)),[^cite_note-79] Rael Braverman (husband of the former [Home Secretary](149104.md) [Suella Braverman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suella_Braverman)),[^cite_note-80] the actress and singer [Holly Valance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holly_Valance) and [Nick Candy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nick_Candy) (the billionaire luxury property developer and former Conservative donor).[^cite_note-81][^cite_note-82]

In December 2024, it was reported that American businessman [Elon Musk](909036.md) was planning a \$100 million donation to the Reform UK party to influence the next UK election. Farage said that talk of a \$100m donation to his party was "pure speculation". That month Farage travelled to Florida, US, to meet Musk, a trip partly financed by his friend [George Cottrell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Cottrell), who had served a prison sentence for fraud in 2017.

On 26 December 2024, Reform UK claimed to have overtaken the Conservatives and become the UK's second-largest party, behind Labour, in terms of size.[^cite_note-Ref/Con_membership_National-83] [Kemi Badenoch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kemi_Badenoch), the leader of the [Conservative Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_Party_\(UK\)), later claimed on [X](9988187.md) that Reform's totals were faked. Following this, Reform invited the *[Financial Times](136566.md)*, [Sky News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sky_News), *[The Spectator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Spectator)* and *[The Daily Telegraph](23797577.md)* to inspect their website's front and back-end code and the underlying data of the party's numbers. Each media outlet subsequently confirmed the ticker to be accurate.[^cite_note-84][^cite_note-85][^cite_note-86][^cite_note-87] Farage refuted Badenoch's claim, stating that the allegations were "disgraceful" and threatened legal action should Badenoch not apologise.[^cite_note-88]

### 2025

On 5 January 2025, the American businessman [Elon Musk](909036.md) publicly urged Farage to step down as leader of Reform UK, marking a sudden withdrawal of support. Musk had previously supported Farage and been photographed with him, but later tweeted "The Reform Party needs a new leader. Farage doesn't have what it takes". The withdrawal of support came after Farage disagreed with and distanced himself from comments made by Musk supporting Robinson, who was jailed for [contempt of court](7201.md).[^cite_note-89] Two days later Farage said that he aimed to "mend fences" with Musk, whom he referred to as a "heroic figure".[^cite_note-90]



Farage speaking at the [Conservative Political Action Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_Political_Action_Conference) (CPAC) in the United States, February 2025

On 3 February 2025, Reform topped a national [YouGov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/YouGov) poll for the first time.[^cite_note-91] On 20 February 2025, following a September 2024 promise by Farage to hand control of the party to its members and give up his ownership of the party, the party ownership was transferred to Reform 2025 Limited, a [company limited by guarantee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Company_limited_by_guarantee) with Farage and Yusuf as directors. Yusuf stated that "We are assembling the governing board, in line with the constitution. This was an important step in professionalising the party as we prepare for government."[^cite_note-RU-92]

In March 2025, Jack Aaron, a parliamentary candidate for the party at the 2024 general election, was appointed as head of vetting for the party.[^cite_note-o009-93][^cite_note-bbc-c871wzx8r47o-94] In that same year Lowe was suspended from the party due to allegations of bullying office staff.[^cite_note-95]

In May 2025 [Sarah Pochin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarah_Pochin) was elected as the party's fifth MP at the [by-election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Runcorn_and_Helsby_by-election) in [Runcorn and Helsby](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Runcorn_and_Helsby), with a majority of 6.[^cite_note-96]

The party placed first, winning the most seats, and took control of 10 local authorities and two mayoralties.[^cite_note-\:0-97] At 30%, Reform's projected national vote share (PNS) was higher than UKIP's 23% at the [2013 local elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2013_United_Kingdom_local_elections), representing the first set of local elections since PNS began to be calculated in which neither the Conservative nor Labour parties received the highest vote share.[^cite_note-\:0-97]

Also in May 2025, analysis by the *[Financial Times](136566.md)* of data from a More In Common survey showed that the projected Reform vote share had a strong correlation with poor social mobility in a constituency, as measured by the educational and early career achievement of those receiving [free school meals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/School_meal#United_Kingdom), with no correlation for the Conservatives and [Liberal Democrats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberal_Democrats_\(UK\)), and a weak positive correlation for Labour. Social mobility is lowest in the constituencies with the highest Leave vote in the [2016 EU referendum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_United_Kingdom_European_Union_membership_referendum) – 27 of the 30 seats with lowest social mobility voted Leave – and highest in constituencies with the highest foreign-born population.[^cite_note-98]

Later in May 2025, a [far-right](3884621.md) influencer, David Clews, and the founder of the far-right organisation [Patriotic Alternative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patriotic_Alternative), [Mark Collett](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_Collett) (both of whom formerly worked for the fascist [British National Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_National_Party)), both called on their supporters to "infiltrate" Reform UK and move it politically further right and in support of extremist views.[^cite_note-PA-99] Clews claimed that he has sympathisers in Reform UK who are branch chairs and who have been on Reform UK candidate lists. A Reform spokesman said the far-right would never be welcome in the party and a "stringent vetting process" was in place.[^cite_note-PA-99]

On 5 June 2025, Yusuf resigned his position as Chairman of Reform UK, stating on Twitter: "I no longer believe working to get a Reform government elected is a good use of my time, and hereby resign the office."[^cite_note-100] It came hours after Pochin's call for a national ban on the wearing of [burqas](165826.md), which led to media speculation that Yusuf's resignation had been as a result of the question and a statement by Reform that it was not official party policy.[^cite_note-\:1-101][^cite_note-102] Yusuf said he had not been informed of Pochin's plans to call for a ban and said it was "dumb" for her to call for a measure which went against Reform policy.[^cite_note-\:1-101][^cite_note-103] Yusuf returned to Reform UK 48 hours in a different capacity after resigning, saying his resignation "was a decision born of exhaustion" and was a "mistake".[^cite_note-BBC-cwyv040rnqzo-104] In a subsequent interview with *[The Sunday Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Sunday_Times)* Yusuf stated that his intervention over the burqa question had been an "error" and that if he were an MP he would "probably" vote in favour of banning the burqa along with other face coverings in public.[^cite_note-105][^cite_note-106] The former deputy leader [David Bull](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Bull_\(politician\)) was later announced as Yusuf's successor as chairman.[^cite_note-107]

Inspired by Musk's [Department of Government Efficiency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Department_of_Government_Efficiency) in US President [Donald Trump's administration](78298108.md), a unit with the title *Department of Government Efficiency* was established by Farage and Yusuf in June 2025 after the party gained control of a number of councils in the 2025 local elections.[^cite_note-108][^cite_note-BBC-cwyv040rnqzo-104] The unit's remit only focused on [Kent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kent_County_Council) and [Lancashire County Councils](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lancashire_County_Council) with plans to extend to [West Northamptonshire Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Northamptonshire_Council). The unit's "head", the tech entrepreneur Nathaniel Fried, resigned alongside Yusuf on 6 June 2025, less than a week after starting the role, saying that as Yusuf had appointed him it was "appropriate for me to leave with him".[^cite_note-localgov-62500-109][^cite_note-110] On 7 June 2025 Yusuf announced he was returning to working with Reform UK, now as the new leader of the unit.[^cite_note-BBC-cwyv040rnqzo-104][^cite_note-111][^cite_note-112]

In June 2025, Reform also contested the [Hamilton, Larkhall and Stonehouse by-election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Hamilton,_Larkhall_and_Stonehouse_by-election) and recorded their strongest result to date in an election for the Scottish Parliament by finishing in third place (behind [Scottish Labour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Labour) and the [Scottish National Party](28887.md)) with 26.1% of the vote. The election was described by journalists and the political scientist [Sir John Curtice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Curtice) as a breakthrough for the party in Scotland.[^cite_note-113][^cite_note-114][^cite_note-115]

At the end of June 2025, two political groups were launched by prominent former Reform UK members, each positioning themselves as an alternative to Reform UK. [Advance UK](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Advance_UK) is a creation of [Ben Habib](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ben_Habib), former Brexit Party MEP and deputy leader of Reform UK, while [Restore Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Restore_Britain) is a creation of Lowe.[^cite_note-116] Both groups were launched on the same day, 30 June.[^cite_note-117] On 5 July 2025 the MP James McMurdock suspended himself from the party as "a precautionary measure" due to a pending investigation into him for previous property tax offences during the COVID-19 pandemic.[^cite_note-118] McMurdock was cleared by the [parliamentary standards commissioner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliamentary_Commissioner_for_Standards) of the allegation but decided not to return to Reform UK and instead remain as an independent MP.[^cite_note-119][^cite_note-120] The party announced the same month that it would replace its existing vetting process with a looser "common-sense vetting" approach, and has suggested that candidates rejected by the previous vetting process re-apply, with re-applications to be treated as priority cases.[^cite_note-121][^cite_note-122]



[David Jones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Jones_\(Clwyd_West_MP\)) is among the former [Conservative Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_Party_\(UK\)) cabinet members to have defected to Reform UK.

[^cite_note-123]

In July 2025, [Paul Nuttall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Nuttall) was appointed as Reform UK's deputy chairman.[^cite_note-124]

In August 2025, Mick Barton, the Reform UK leader of [Nottinghamshire County Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nottinghamshire_County_Council) took the unprecedented step of banning the local newspaper, *[Nottingham Post](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nottingham_Post)*, from talking to himself or any Reform UK councillors and said that the authority would stop sending press releases to the publication and it would not be invited to council events. Barton accused the paper of "consistently misrepresenting our policies, actions or intentions" and said the ban was "not about silencing journalism", but "about upholding the principle that freedom of speech must be paired with responsibility and honesty".[^cite_note-125][^cite_note-126] Deputy leader Richard Tice defended the policy when challenged about it being undemocratic to ban media.[^cite_note-127] On 17 September 2025, Mason Humberstone a member of Stevenage Borough Council, became the first Labour Party councillor to defect to Reform UK.[^cite_note-128]



[Danny Kruger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Danny_Kruger), the [MP](3776360.md) for [East Wiltshire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Wiltshire) who defected from the Conservatives to Reform in September 2025, was the first sitting MP to defect to Reform from another party.

[^cite_note-129]

On 15 September, [Danny Kruger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Danny_Kruger), the Conservative MP for [East Wiltshire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Wiltshire), defected to Reform. In a press conference after his defection was announced, Kruger stated "the Conservatives are over" and that he was "honoured" to have been asked to help prepare Reform for government, adding that he hoped Farage would be the next prime minister.[^cite_note-ReferenceA-130] On 26 September, Reform UK announced it had reached 250,000 paid-up members, more than double that of the Conservatives at 123,000 members, and just shy of 60,000 short of Labour, which had the largest number of members at 309,000.[^cite_note-131] On the same day, [Nathan Gill](42863259.md), an ex-Reform politician and former leader of Reform Wales between March and May 2021, who quit the party later that year, pleaded guilty for accepting bribes from pro-Russian journalist and former member of the Ukrainian Parliament pro-Russian '[Opposition Platform — For Life](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opposition_Platform_%E2%80%94_For_Life)' party [Oleh Voloshyn](56069568.md), at the [Old Bailey](229046.md) in London. Gill pleaded guilty to eight counts of bribery. The charges related to payments of money he accepted from Voloshyn between 2018 and 2019 while serving as a Brexit Party [MEP](258909.md) in the [European Parliament](9581.md), in exchange for making specific pro-Russian statements directed by Voloshyn in the European parliament and media.[^cite_note-132][^cite_note-133][^cite_note-134] Gill was sentenced on 21 November 2025 to ten years and six months in prison.[^cite_note-135] Reform UK condemned Gill's actions and Farage described him as a "bad apple".[^cite_note-136]

In September, Tice took part in "Britain's March Against Antisemitism", at which he said, "I don't want a single Jewish person to feel like they have to leave. We need to stand united, united as proud Britons."[^cite_note-137] The following month,[^cite_note-138] a [YouGov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/YouGov) poll found that 46% of white British respondents viewed the party and its policies as racist, compared with 36% who regarded the party as "generally not racist". Among respondents from ethnic minority backgrounds, 13% had a favourable opinion while 80% had a negative view.[^cite_note-139]

In December 2025, the [Electoral Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electoral_Commission_\(United_Kingdom\)) reported that Reform UK had received over £10 million in donations in the third quarter of 2025.[^cite_note-140] These donations included, but were not limited to, a £9 million donation by [Christopher Harborne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christopher_Harborne)[^cite_note-141] and donations of £250,000 and £240,000 by [Nick Candy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nick_Candy).[^cite_note-142][^cite_note-143] In the same month, a report by *[The Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Times)* said Reform had grown into the largest political party in Britain and overtaken Labour by membership numbers.[^cite_note-144][^cite_note-145]

Later that month, Essex Police began assessing allegations that Reform UK overspent on Farage's campaign to become an MP in [Clacton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clacton_\(UK_Parliament_constituency\)) in the [2024 United Kingdom general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_United_Kingdom_general_election). The claims were made by Richard Everett, a former Reform UK councillor. A Reform spokesperson denied the party had broken electoral law, saying the "inaccurate claims come from a disgruntled former councillor". Essex Police later said that an investigation could not take place because more than a year had passed since the alleged offence. The [Electoral Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electoral_Commission_\(United_Kingdom\)) said it would take no further action on the allegations, stating: "We did not identify credible evidence of potential offences of electoral law".

### 2026

On 7 January 2026, [Laila Cunningham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laila_Cunningham) was announced as the Reform candidate for the [2028 London mayoral election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2028_London_mayoral_election).[^cite_note-146][^cite_note-147] On 12 January 2026, former Conservative [Chancellor of the Exchequer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chancellor_of_the_Exchequer) [Nadhim Zahawi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nadhim_Zahawi) joined Reform UK.[^cite_note-148] Three days later, former [Shadow Secretary of State for Justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shadow_Secretary_of_State_for_Justice) and [Shadow Lord Chancellor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shadow_Lord_Chancellor) and [Conservative Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_Party_\(UK\)) immigration minister [Robert Jenrick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Jenrick) joined Reform UK after being expelled from the Conservative Party, bringing the party's representation in the House of Commons to six MPs.[^cite_note-149][^cite_note-150] On 18 January 2026, Conservative MP for [Romford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romford_\(constituency\)), [Andrew Rosindell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_Rosindell), announced he had switched allegiances to Reform and became the party's seventh Member of Parliament.[^cite_note-151] On 26 January 2026, Conservative MP for [Fareham and Waterlooville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fareham_and_Waterlooville) and former [Home Secretary](149104.md) [Suella Braverman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suella_Braverman) also defected to Reform, becoming the party's eighth MP.[^cite_note-\:5-152]

On 27 January 2026, [GB News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GB_News) presenter and author Matthew Goodwin was announced as Reform UK's candidate for the [Gorton and Denton by-election](82184590.md). He was described by the Liberal Democrats, who also had a candidate in the election, as being racist for suggesting that some people from ethnic minority backgrounds born in the UK were not necessarily British.[^cite_note-153][^cite_note-154] Goodwin stated the media reports were misleading, and that he said that second generation immigrants committing terrorist acts such as the [Manchester Arena bombing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manchester_Arena_bombing) are not "as British as" their victims.[^cite_note-men-wilkinson-155]

On 4 February 2026, *The National* reported that the Reform UK treasurer, [Nick Candy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nick_Candy), was named in the [Epstein files](80415168.md).[^cite_note-156] On 10 February 2026, the *Clacton Gazette* reported that Farage said there was no wrongdoing because Candy “never had anything to do with Epstein.”[^cite_note-Rees-157]

On 13 February 2026, former Reform MP [Rupert Lowe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rupert_Lowe) announced the creation of his own political party, [Restore Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Restore_Britain), which had existed since 30 June 2025 as a [pressure group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pressure_group).[^cite_note-158] Shortly after, a number of Reform councilors defected to the new party.[^cite_note-159]

On 17 February 2026, Nigel Farage announced the creation of his [Reform UK frontbench team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frontbench_team_of_Nigel_Farage).[^cite_note-\:02-160][^cite_note-\:12-161] In the May [2026 local elections](79139854.md), Reform UK won over 1,400 seats (out of 4754). In the [Scottish Parliament election](65934215.md), Reform won 17 MSPs (out of 129), and in the [Welsh Senedd election](68245236.md), they won 34 MSs (out of 96).[^cite_note-162]

### Financial allegations

Main article: [Reform UK financial allegations](83649192.md)

In 2026, the finances of Reform UK and Nigel Farage at the time of the [2024 United Kingdom general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_United_Kingdom_general_election) came under scrutiny following reports by *[The Guardian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Guardian)* and *[The Sunday Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Sunday_Times)* that Farage had failed to declare donations from [Christopher Harborne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christopher_Harborne) and [George Cottrell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Cottrell).[^cite_note-Isaac-1-163][^cite_note-Mason-Wheeler-164][^cite_note-165] Farage and representatives from Reform said that Farage did not need to declare these donations as they were gifts given to him before he became an MP.[^cite_note-Isaac-2-166][^cite_note-Cooke-1-167] Donations made by Cottrell and his mother Fiona to Reform UK during the same time period are also under investigation, with [Richard Tice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Tice) and [George Cottrell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Cottrell) both denying that there was anything wrong with these donations.[^cite_note-168][^cite_note-169] Farage subsequently resigned from parliament in order to [fight a by-election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_Clacton_by-election) in his [Clacton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clacton_\(constituency\)) constituency in which he was duly re-elected.[^cite_note-Gutteridge-170] As of July, the [Metropolitan Police](192450.md) has been investigating four donations from George Cottrell for more than a year. Two people have been interviewed under caution.[^cite_note-171][^cite_note-172][^cite_note-173]

In September 2026, Reform Head of Policy [James Orr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Orr_\(philosopher\)) and [Dan Jukes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dan_Jukes), an aide to Nigel Farage, appeared in undercover reporting showing Orr and Jukes arrange for an American company to fund three polls on behalf of the party, which would break campaign finance laws.[^cite_note-\:42-174] Orr told the would-be donor: "We just don't have British resident donors, British entities, that would support us. That's the grim reality ... there are quite a few people in your position ... who can't declare \[funding]."[^cite_note-\:42-174][^cite_note-175] Jukes and Orr separately proposed ways that donations from inadmissible donors could be hidden through a British intermediary,[^cite_note-\:42-174][^cite_note-m787-176] which Jukes said that the party had done several times already.[^cite_note-177] Orr said that "the reality is we could easily get away with it", and that the [Electoral Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electoral_Commission_\(United_Kingdom\)) was "so dumb and ineffective".[^cite_note-\:42-174] Jukes said: "Those little clandestine projects are so valuable."[^cite_note-\:42-174] Of the payments, Orr said that Farage "doesn't know, won't know. It's the kind of thing he would rather not know."[^cite_note-178][^cite_note-179] After resigning as MP amid allegations regarding the Harborne gift, Farage met the undercover foreign donor in person to discuss a potential £500,000 donation via a British intermediary, and thanked him for paying for the polling.[^cite_note-\:42-174]

Following the report, both Orr and Jukes stepped down and Reform announced an internal probe into the matter.[^cite_note-n657-180] Jukes denied breaking any law. Orr said he would co-operate fully with Reform's internal investigation.[^cite_note-\:21-181] Reform UK chair Lee Anderson said that there was "no chance" of either Jukes or Orr returning.[^cite_note-182] Farage told the BBC that he "wasn't even listening" during the conversation with the donor.[^cite_note-\:21-181] The Metropolitan Police launched a criminal investigation into the donations.[^cite_note-183]

Amid the allegations, Reform UK's standing in the polls dropped to 24% in September 2026 from 31% the September before, according to averages from Sky News.[^cite_note-\:110-184][^cite_note-185] Nigel Farage's net approval fell from -2% to -30% over the same period.[^cite_note-\:110-184] [YouGov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/YouGov) recorded their highest disapproval rating for Farage in over ten years of tracking at 69%.[^cite_note-186][^cite_note-187]

### Press bans

In a number of counties Reform UK has banned its councillors from speaking to local press.[^cite_note-\:17-188][^cite_note-\:18-189][^cite_note-\:19-190] After taking control of [Nottinghamshire County Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nottinghamshire_County_Council) in May 2025, Reform UK banned its councillors from speaking to the *[Nottingham Post](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nottingham_Post)*, and council officials were ordered not to send it press releases.[^cite_note-\:18-189] According to [*The* *Times*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Times) columnist Sean O'Neill, "this ominous move ... represents an attack on the democratic structures of this country's system of government".[^cite_note-\:18-189] In October 2025, Reform also banned local councillors from speaking to the *[Leicester Gazette](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leicester_Gazette)* and the *Kent Current*, branding the *Gazette* as "fake news". *[Nation.Cymru](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nation.Cymru)* news editor Emily Price reported that Reform was refusing to speak to their journalists, which she described as an "undemocratic [Donald Trump-style](77534993.md) wall of silence". The *Nottingham Post* initiated legal action against Reform UK to reverse its ban.[^cite_note-191]

In May 2026, Reform UK councillors were told not to engage with local press after winning council control in Suffolk and Swindon.[^cite_note-192][^cite_note-\:19-190] Ipswich Reform chair Shayne Pooley publicly stated that councillors will not respond to any media except [GB News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GB_News).[^cite_note-\:17-188] On election day, only one Reform candidate in Ipswich agreed to be interviewed by the BBC.[^cite_note-\:19-190]

After news broke on 29 April 2026 of a [£5 million gift from Christopher Harborne](83649192.md#Christopher_Harborne_donation), Farage scaled back press conferences, holding only two since then (one open only to local press) as of mid-June, having held 20 between January and April.[^cite_note-193][^cite_note-\:03-194]

In September 2026, Reform UK revoked the press permits for its annual conference of at least eight journalists, including the *Guardian* journalist, Anna Isaac, who broke the news of the Harborne gift.[^cite_note-195][^cite_note-196][^cite_note-\:20-197] [National Union of Journalists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Union_of_Journalists) general secretary Laura Davison stated: "Reports that Reform UK has chosen to ban journalists who have scrutinised the party from attending its conference are frustrating and concerning."[^cite_note-198] Conservative leader Kemi Badenoch said this indicated that Reform would be "authoritarian and using state power actually to pursue opponents" if elected to government.[^cite_note-\:20-197]

As of September 2026, Richard Tice and his lawyers have ignored over 40 attempts by *[Sunday Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Sunday_Times)* *[Insight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Sunday_Times)* editor [Gabriel Pogrund](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gabriel_Pogrund) to contact him regarding his tax affairs.[^cite_note-199]

## Representation

Main article: [List of Reform UK politicians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Reform_UK_politicians)

See also: [List of Conservative Party defections to Reform UK](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Conservative_Party_defections_to_Reform_UK)

### House of Commons

Main article: [List of Reform UK MPs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Reform_UK_MPs)



Reform UK MPs [Nigel Farage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nigel_Farage) (second from right), [Richard Tice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Tice) (second from left) with former Reform UK MPs [Rupert Lowe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rupert_Lowe) (right), [James McMurdock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_McMurdock) (left) in 2024

[Lee Anderson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lee_Anderson), who was elected as the Conservative Party MP for [Ashfield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashfield_\(constituency\)) in the [2019 general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_United_Kingdom_general_election), defected to Reform UK in March 2024, giving the party its first MP.[^cite_note-bbc-lee-200] He was re-elected in the [2024 general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_United_Kingdom_general_election) and joined by [Nigel Farage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nigel_Farage), [Richard Tice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Tice), [Rupert Lowe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rupert_Lowe), and [James McMurdock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_McMurdock), representing [Clacton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clacton_\(constituency\)), [Boston and Skegness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boston_and_Skegness), [Great Yarmouth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Yarmouth_\(constituency\)), and [South Basildon and East Thurrock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Basildon_and_East_Thurrock) respectively.[^cite_note-201][^cite_note-202]

Rupert Lowe was suspended from the party in March 2025 after criticising Farage's leadership, lowering the party's MPs to four.[^cite_note-203][^cite_note-204] The number increased to five again after [Sarah Pochin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarah_Pochin) won the [2025 Runcorn and Helsby by-election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Runcorn_and_Helsby_by-election).[^cite_note-205] McMurdock stepped down from the party in July 2025, after previously admitting to business misconduct.[^cite_note-206]

On 15 September, [Danny Kruger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Danny_Kruger), the Conservative MP for [East Wiltshire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Wiltshire), defected to Reform, bringing Reform's total MPs back up to 5 again after McMurdock stepped down in July.[^cite_note-ReferenceA-130] In January 2026, Conservative MPs [Robert Jenrick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Jenrick), [Andrew Rosindell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_Rosindell) and [Suella Braverman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suella_Braverman) defected to Reform, bringing the party's seats to eight with the additions of [Newark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newark_\(constituency\)), [Romford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romford_\(constituency\)) and [Fareham and Waterlooville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fareham_and_Waterlooville).[^cite_note-207][^cite_note-208][^cite_note-\:5-152]

### House of Lords

In December 2025, [Malcolm Offord](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malcolm_Offord) defected from the [Conservatives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Conservatives) to Reform UK, becoming its first and only peer in the [House of Lords](13658.md), he retired his position in the Lords after being appointed the party's leader in Scotland.[^cite_note-209][^cite_note-210]

### European Parliament

In February 2019, nine MEPs, who had left UKIP in opposition to [Gerard Batten](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gerard_Batten)'s leadership, joined the party;[^cite_note-Newsnight-16] by mid-April 2019, the number had increased to 14, all being members of the [Europe of Freedom and Direct Democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Europe_of_Freedom_and_Direct_Democracy) (EFDD) group in the European Parliament.[^cite_note-europarl-211]
