| [Jane Collins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jane_Collins) | [Yorkshire and the Humber](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yorkshire_and_the_Humber_\(European_Parliament_constituency\)) | 1 July 2014 | 15 April 2019 (2019-04-15) |
| [Margot Parker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Margot_Parker) | [East Midlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Midlands_\(European_Parliament_constituency\)) | 1 July 2014 | 15 April 2019 (2019-04-15) |
| [Jonathan Arnott](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jonathan_Arnott) | [North East England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_East_England_\(European_Parliament_constituency\)) | 1 July 2014 | 17 April 2019 (2019-04-17) |
| [Ray Finch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ray_Finch) | [South East England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_East_England_\(European_Parliament_constituency\)) | 1 July 2014 | 17 April 2019 (2019-04-17) |

Only three of these incumbent MEPs − Farage, Gill and Bullock − were selected to stand for the Brexit Party in the 2019 election,[^cite_note-212] which took place on 23 May 2019. Twenty-nine Brexit Party MEPs were elected to the [European Parliament](9581.md), including Tice and the former Conservative MP [Ann Widdecombe](2422.md), while Farage, [Nathan Gill](42863259.md) and [Jonathan Bullock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jonathan_Bullock) kept their seats.[^cite_note-213] BBC News described the Brexit Party, which gained 31.6% of the vote in the UK, as "the clear winner in the UK's European elections."[^cite_note-214]

The Brexit Party MEPs were not members of a group in the Parliament. The MEP [Andrew England Kerr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_England_Kerr) was expelled from the party on 29 September 2019 over a potential conflict of interest. Farage explained that England Kerr made "comments about a business and a product that he has a direct financial investment in and we think that is unacceptable."[^cite_note-215] The MEP [Louis Stedman-Bryce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis_Stedman-Bryce) resigned on 19 November 2019 in response to "The Brexit Party's recent decision to select a Scottish candidate who has openly posted homophobic views".[^cite_note-216]

### London Assembly

In the [2024 London Assembly election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_London_Assembly_election), [Alex Wilson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alex_Wilson_\(British_politician\)) was elected as a London-wide member for the [London Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/London_Assembly).[^cite_note-London_Elects_2024-60]

On 4 October 2025, [Keith Prince](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Keith_Prince), the [Conservative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_Party_\(UK\)) London Assembly member for [Havering and Redbridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Havering_and_Redbridge_\(London_Assembly_constituency\)), defected to Reform.[^cite_note-217]

### Senedd

See also: [Reform UK Wales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reform_UK_Wales)

On 15 May 2019, four Members originally elected or co-opted for UKIP ([Caroline Jones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caroline_Jones_\(politician\)), [Mandy Jones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandy_Jones_\(politician\)), [David Rowlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Rowlands_\(politician\)) and [Mark Reckless](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_Reckless)) joined the Brexit Party,[^cite_note-218] with Reckless being appointed as leader of their group,[^cite_note-219] which was known as *Plaid Brexit* in [Welsh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Welsh_language).[^cite_note-220] In May 2020 Reckless said that Nigel Farage is "consulted over key decisions... but he doesn't micro-manage us here", and that in the [2021 Senedd election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Senedd_election) it would campaign to scrap the current system of devolution and replace it with a directly elected first minister accountable to Welsh MPs.[^cite_note-221] This policy announcement triggered the departure from the party's group in the Senedd of Caroline Jones, Mandy Jones and David Rowlands. They formed a new members group, the [Independent Alliance for Reform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independent_Alliance_for_Reform), which sought to reform rather than abolish the Senedd.[^cite_note-222][^cite_note-223] The remaining Brexit Party Senedd group member, Mark Reckless, left to join the [Abolish the Welsh Assembly Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abolish_the_Welsh_Assembly_Party).[^cite_note-224]

Reform UK contested the [2021 Senedd election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Senedd_election) on a platform of ending lockdowns, investing in the NHS, giving parents greater control over education, building the [M4 relief road](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M4_relief_road), and cutting local government,[^cite_note-225] but did not win any seats, although they got a one percent vote share for regional and constituency lists.[^cite_note-226]



[Dan Thomas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dan_Thomas) with Reform UK [Members of the Senedd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Member_of_the_Senedd).

On 22 July 2025, [Laura Anne Jones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laura_Anne_Jones), then a [Welsh Conservative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Welsh_Conservatives) member of the Senedd for [South Wales East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Wales_East_\(Senedd_electoral_region\)), defected to the party, becoming their first representative in the [Senedd](238480.md) since the party was renamed to Reform UK.[^cite_note-227] This decision came while she was under investigation by the Senedd standards watchdog due to reports of bullying and false expenses claims.[^cite_note-228]

On 20 January 2026, [James Evans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Evans_\(Welsh_politician\)), then a Welsh Conservative member of the Senedd for [Brecon and Randorshire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brecon_and_Radnorshire_\(Senedd_constituency\)), had the whip removed following Evans confirming to [Darren Millar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Darren_Millar) that he was in talks with Reform UK about a possible defection.[^cite_note-229] Evans subsequently sat as an independent for 16 days before announcing his defection to Reform UK on 5 February 2026.[^cite_note-230]

On 7 May 2026, following the [2026 Senedd election](68245236.md), Reform UK became the second largest party in the Senedd after [Plaid Cymru](53343.md), winning 34 of the 96 seats up for election.[^cite_note-231] [Dan Thomas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dan_Thomas) subsequently became the [leader of the opposittion in Wales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leader_of_the_Opposition_\(Wales\)) and formed the [Thomas shadow cabinet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_shadow_cabinet).[^cite_note-232]

On 15 September 2026, Dan Thomas resigned as leader of Reform UK Wales after being arrested on suspicion of assault and controlling or coercive behavior. Thomas said that he had "done nothing wrong". [Gwent Police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gwent_Police) confirmed that a 45-year-old man from [Aberbargoed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aberbargoed) had been [released on bail](170652.md).[^cite_note-233] That same day, [Sarah Cooper-Lesadd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarah_Cooper-Lesadd), then a Reform UK Wales member of the Senedd for [Pen-y-bont Bro Morgannwg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pen-y-bont_Bro_Morgannwg), defected to Plaid Cymru.[^cite_note-234]

On 21 September 2026, [Helen Jenner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helen_Jenner) was unveiled as the successor to Dan Thomas following a [leadership contest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_Reform_UK_Wales_leadership_election) by [Jason O'Connell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jason_O'Connell). Jenner had previously served as Thomas' [deputy leader](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deputy_Leader_of_Reform_UK_Wales).[^cite_note-235] The next day, [Laura Anne Jones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laura_Anne_Jones) was announced as Jenner's deputy leader.[^cite_note-236]

### Scottish Parliament

See also: [Reform UK Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reform_UK_Scotland)



[Malcolm Offord](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malcolm_Offord) leads Reform UK in Scotland

On 11 January 2021, [Michelle Ballantyne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michelle_Ballantyne), an MSP for [South Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Scotland_\(Scottish_Parliament_electoral_region\)), joined Reform UK. She first sat as a Conservative but left the party in 2020.[^cite_note-237][^cite_note-238] Ballantyne continued to sit with the party until the [2021 Scottish Parliament election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Scottish_Parliament_election) in May, when she lost her seat to a candidate from the [Scottish Conservatives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Conservatives). She resigned as the party's leader in Scotland in February 2022.[^cite_note-National_Feb_2022-34][^cite_note-Herald_Feb_2022-35]

On 27 August 2025, [Graham Simpson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Graham_Simpson_\(politician\)), an MSP for [Central Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Scotland_\(Scottish_Parliament_electoral_region\)), defected from the Conservatives and joined Reform.[^cite_note-239]

On 7 May 2026, in the [2026 Scottish Parliament election](65934215.md), Reform UK became the joint-second largest party in the Scottish Parliament, tied with [Scottish Labour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Labour) and behind the [Scottish National Party](28887.md), with 17 of the 129 seats up for election.[^cite_note-240]

### Police and crime commissioners

On 4 August 2025, [Rupert Matthews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rupert_Matthews), the [Leicestershire Police and Crime Commissioner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leicestershire_Police_and_Crime_Commissioner) and a former MEP for [East Midlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Midlands_\(European_Parliament_constituency\)), defected from the Conservatives and joined Reform.[^cite_note-241]

In July 2026, Reform candidate [Colin Sutton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colin_Sutton_\(police_and_crime_commissioner\)) won the [2026 Norfolk Police and Crime Commissioner by-election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_Norfolk_Police_and_Crime_Commissioner_by-election), gaining the seat from Labour.[^cite_note-242]

### Local government





Map of council control after the United Kingdom local elections in [2025](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_United_Kingdom_local_elections) (left) and [2026](79139854.md) (right). Reform UK in light blue

As of March 2024, two-thirds of Reform UK's local councillors were former councillors of the Conservative party who defected over to Reform UK.[^cite_note-Tory_to_Reform-243] In October 2024, Farage called on Conservative Party councillors to join Reform UK, saying that he was contacting over a thousand of them and that "a huge number of them genuinely agree with us and what we stand for".[^cite_note-244]

Reform won a by-election in the Marton ward of Blackpool Council on 3 October 2024, with its vote share rising from 9.5% (in the 2023 election) to 38.8%.[^cite_note-245] In October 2024, two [Scottish Conservative Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Conservatives) councillors, Mark Findlater and Laurie Carnie, serving on [Aberdeenshire Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aberdeenshire_Council) defected to the party and became Reform UK's first local representatives in Scotland.[^cite_note-246][^cite_note-247] As of 17 March 2025, prior to the local elections of that year, 15 of the 113 Reform councillors had been won through elections, with the remainder defecting from other parties, the majority of which were from the Conservatives.[^cite_note-bbc-c871wzx8r47o-94] In June 2025, a [Scottish Labour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Labour) councillor, Jamie McGuire, from [Renfrewshire Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renfrewshire_Council) defected to Reform along with a third former Scottish Conservatives councillor, Duncan Massey, from Aberdeen.[^cite_note-248][^cite_note-249]

Reform polled in first place and won 677 seats in the [2025 local elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_United_Kingdom_local_elections). Within six weeks the number of Reform councillors had fallen to 668 due to five suspensions from the party, and four newly appointed councillors stepping down from the role, which led to two by-election losses (one to the Conservatives and the other to the Lib Dems).[^cite_note-bbc-durham-250][^cite_note-bbc-conservative-win-251] Two further by-elections were scheduled for July and August 2025.[^cite_note-all-candidates-252][^cite_note-durham-bye-253]

Within months of the election, issues emerged for Reform UK party groups on local authorities. On 18 October 2025, *[The Guardian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Guardian)* released video footage of an online meeting of Reform UK councillors where [Linden Kemkaran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Linden_Kemkaran), leader of [Kent County Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kent_County_Council), swore at some of her colleagues who criticised her leadership.[^cite_note-Linden_Kemkaran_Suck_it_uP-254] After the leak she called those responsible cowards.[^cite_note-255] Four councillors were suspended as a result of the leak.[^cite_note-256] On [Cornwall Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cornwall_Council), the leader and deputy leader of the group resigned.[^cite_note-257] Other local authorities where elected councillors who have left include [City of Doncaster Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_of_Doncaster_Council),[^cite_note-258] [Northumberland County Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northumberland_County_Council),[^cite_note-259] [Nottinghamshire County Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nottinghamshire_County_Council),[^cite_note-260] [Staffordshire County Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Staffordshire_County_Council),[^cite_note-261] and [Shropshire Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shropshire_Council).[^cite_note-262]

Reform again polled first in the [2026 local elections](79139854.md), gaining 1,451 seats.[^cite_note-263]

## Ideology and platform

Reform UK is a [radical right](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radical_right_\(Europe\))[^cite_note-Webb2024-264][^cite_note-v704-265][^cite_note-h440-266] and [anti-immigration](6491626.md) political party.[^cite_note-i025-267][^cite_note-d8982-268] It is [right-wing populist](8354382.md)[^cite_note-populist-272][^cite_note-TournierSol2021-273][^cite_note-CombeiReggi2024-274][^cite_note-ColeStaffordHeinz2022-275] and [far-right](221220.md).[^cite_note-hard-right-279][^cite_note-Miori-2025-280][^cite_note-281][^cite_note-r302-282] It is also [neoliberal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neoliberal) populist,[^cite_note-283] [right-wing nationalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Right-wing_nationalist),[^cite_note-Lockard2020-284] and [national conservative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_conservatism).[^cite_note-\:3-285] Reform UK rejects the descriptor of far-right, threatening legal action against media using it,[^cite_note-Naughtie_2024_h130-286] while Farage has personally described Reform as occupying the "genuine [centre-right](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centre-right_politics)" and as a "brand new Conservative movement".[^cite_note-287][^cite_note-288][^cite_note-289][^cite_note-\:7-67]

In March 2024, the [BBC](19344654.md) called the party far-right but soon retracted its statement and apologised to Reform UK, writing that describing the party as far-right "fell short of our usual editorial standards".[^cite_note-290] Commenting on the incident, professor of politics [Tim Bale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tim_Bale) wrote that labelling Reform UK as far-right is unhelpful, and that it "causes too visceral a reaction and at the same time is too broad to be meaningful", as an umbrella term. Bale stated that the importance of distinguishing between the "[extreme right](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extreme_right)" and "populist radical right", and stated that parties described as far right should instead be "more precisely labelled".[^cite_note-291] Bale later described party leader Farage as the "British representative of the populist radical right in Europe", while being placed "on the more moderate side of far-right parties in Europe."[^cite_note-292] In 2025 Bale stated that "most political scientists" would say that Reform is a far-right party, "if you accept that as the umbrella term and then say they're populist radical right".[^cite_note-293]

In *[Parliamentary Affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliamentary_Affairs)*, Bennie & Widfeldt described the party as fitting "into a broader European family of radical right parties working within democratic structures", with the radical right being a subset of far-right.[^cite_note-\:13-294] In [*British Politics*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Politics_\(journal\)), Hayton wrote that the party had evolved into a "fully-fledged right-wing populist party" that "can be most sensibly classified as a populist radical right party", having moved beyond the single-issue politics of Brexit while retaining a Eurosceptic legacy. Hayton placed this as "outside of the mainstream right and as part of the far right, but distinguishes it from the extreme right elements of the far right."[^cite_note-\:3-285] In the *[Journal of Contemporary European Studies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Journal_of_Contemporary_European_Studies)*, Shuttleworth, Brown & Mondon stated that the categorisation of Reform UK as far-right had been regularly left out from academic and public discourse, despite its "clear credentials", and questioned whether the BBC apology or the party's opposition to label was a reason for such exclusion.[^cite_note-295] Since the apology, the party has also been described as far-right in the *[Journal of European Public Policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Journal_of_European_Public_Policy)*,[^cite_note-296] *[Journal of Cultural Economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Journal_of_Cultural_Economy)*,[^cite_note-297] *[Journal of Elections, Public Opinion and Parties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Journal_of_Elections,_Public_Opinion_and_Parties)*,[^cite_note-298] *[Ethnic and Racial Studies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_and_Racial_Studies)*,[^cite_note-299] [*Nations and Nationalism*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nations_and_Nationalism_\(journal\))*[^cite_note-\:4-300]* and *[Studies in Conflict & Terrorism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Studies_in_Conflict_&_Terrorism)*.[^cite_note-301]

In the *[British Journal of Politics and International Relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Journal_of_Politics_and_International_Relations)*, Newton categorised the party as right-wing populist that strategically utilises radical right populist ideology, differentiating it from minor [far-right parties in the UK](3884621.md) engaged in similar anti-migration activism.[^cite_note-302] Writing in *[The Political Quarterly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Political_Quarterly)*, Wraight described Reform UK as having rallied voters behind their brand of "[little Englander](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Little_Englander) conservatism", as populist rivals to the right of the Conservative Party.[^cite_note-303] Writing in the magazine *[The Spectator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Spectator)*, [Ross Clark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ross_Clark_\(journalist\)) argued that Reform had become a left-wing party, by attracting disillusioned Labour voters with stances on restoring welfare benefits, nationalising the steel industry with 50% of utilities and increasing government spending (including the NHS).[^cite_note-304]

### 2019 European Parliament election platform as the Brexit Party

The party's constitution was published by the Electoral Commission as a result of a freedom of information request in May 2019.[^cite_note-305] It described the party as seeking to "promote and encourage those who aspire to improve their personal situation and those who seek to be self-reliant, whilst providing protection for those genuinely in need; favour the ability of individuals to make decisions in respect of themselves; seek to diminish the role of the State; lower the burden of taxation on individuals and businesses."[^cite_note-business-structure-306]

[Social Democratic Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_Democratic_Party_\(UK,_1990%E2%80%93present\)) politician [Patrick O'Flynn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patrick_O'Flynn), who was elected as a UKIP MEP under Farage's leadership and supported the Brexit Party in the 2019 European elections, commented on the constitution's description of the party as following [classical liberalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_liberalism) and described them as having a [Thatcherite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thatcherism) ideological core.[^cite_note-307] [James Glancy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Glancy), one of the party's MEPs, has compared the party to the [Referendum Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Referendum_Party), being a "united and diverse group of people from different political backgrounds".[^cite_note-308]

The party's first non-Brexit-related policy was announced on 4 June 2019: a proposition to transform [British Steel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Steel_\(2016%E2%80%93present\)) into a partly [worker-owned](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Worker_cooperative) company, in what was described as "a hybrid of Conservative and Labour policy".[^cite_note-309] The party also supported cutting Britain's foreign aid budget, scrapping the proposed [High Speed 2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High_Speed_2) project and introducing free WiFi on all British public transport.[^cite_note-310][^cite_note-311] The party also said it would scrap all interest paid on student tuition fees, reimburse graduates for historic interest payments made on their loans,[^cite_note-312] and pledged to abolish [inheritance tax](5824627.md).[^cite_note-313]

In July 2019, the Brexit Party signed a cross-party declaration alongside the [Liberal Democrats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberal_Democrats_\(UK\)), [Green Party of England and Wales](79787.md), and the [Scottish National Party](28887.md), calling for [first-past-the-post voting](261709.md) to be replaced by a [proportional system](23298.md) for Westminster elections.[^cite_note-314]

### 2019 UK general election platform as the Brexit Party

On 22 November 2019, the Brexit Party set out its policy proposals for the [2019 UK general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_United_Kingdom_general_election). Its key policies for the election included:[^cite_note-BP-24][^cite_note-CP-25]

- No extension to the [Brexit transition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brexit_withdrawal_agreement) period

- No privatisation of the [NHS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Health_Service)

- Reducing immigration

- Cutting [VAT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Value-added_tax_in_the_United_Kingdom) on domestic fuel

- Banning the UK exporting its waste

- Providing free [broadband](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Broadband) in deprived regions

- Scrapping the [television licence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Television_licensing_in_the_United_Kingdom) fee

- Abolishing [inheritance tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inheritance_tax_in_the_United_Kingdom)

- Scrapping [High Speed 2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High_Speed_2) (HS2)

- Abolishing interest on [student loans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Student_loan)

- Changing planning to help house building

- Reforming the [Supreme Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Court_of_the_United_Kingdom)

- [Reform the voting system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electoral_reform) to make it more representative

- Abolish the [House of Lords](13658.md)

- Making MPs who switch parties subject to recall petitions

- Reform the postal voting system to combat fraud

- Introduce [citizens' initiatives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Popular_initiative) to allow people to call [referendums](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Referendums_in_the_United_Kingdom), subject to a 5 million threshold of registered voter signatures and time limitations on repeat votes

### 2020 to 2023 as Reform UK

Following the UK's departure from the European Union on 31 January 2020, Farage sought a new right-wing populist project for the party under its new name of Reform UK, opposing further COVID-19 restrictions, paralleling right-wing populist anti-lockdown sentiments in other countries.[^cite_note-315]

At the party conference in October 2021, leader Richard Tice criticised the Conservative Party as a party of "high tax". He said that his party would stand on a low-tax and low-regulation platform. The party supports raising the threshold at which people start paying income tax from £12,500 to £20,000, and exempting the smallest businesses from corporation tax. He has said that energy companies should be owned by the government or British pension funds to stop profits going abroad.[^cite_note-bbc-tice-attacks-316]

In January 2023, Reform called for an end to foreign ownership of critical national infrastructure such as water, though as part of its plans private firms would continue to supply and distribute water.[^cite_note-317]

### 2024 UK general election platform as Reform UK

On 17 June 2024, Reform UK launched their manifesto, [Our Contract with You](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Our_Contract_with_You), which Farage presented during an interview. The key policy proposals included:

- Tax cuts, including: raising the minimum threshold of [income tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taxation_in_the_United_Kingdom) to £20,000, raising the higher rate threshold from £50,271 to £70,000,

  [^cite_note-Sky_News,_June_2024-318]

  &#x20;abolishing [stamp duty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stamp_duty_in_the_United_Kingdom) for properties below £750,000, and abolishing [taxes on inheritances](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inheritance_tax_in_the_United_Kingdom) below £2 million.

  [^cite_note-ITVrun-319]

  [^cite_note-BBCrun-320]

- Reducing legal immigration by freezing "non-essential" immigration, and eliminating [illegal immigration](7124412.md) by ending the settlement of any illegal immigrants, sending back to France migrants who arrive on boats [crossing the English Channel](59538230.md). To encourage companies to employ British workers, they would raise employers [National Insurance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Insurance) to 20% for foreign workers.

  [^cite_note-ITVrun-319]

  [^cite_note-BBCrun-320]

- Scrapping and rejecting [net zero](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Net-zero_emissions) as "the greatest act of negligence". Reform UK wants to increase drilling for gas and oil, seeing their expansion as growth opportunities.

  [^cite_note-Sky_News,_Reform_UK_policies-321]

  &#x20;It would also "fast-track" clean [nuclear energy](22153.md) and shale gas licences.

  [^cite_note-Daily_Telegraph,_Reform_UK_policies-322]

  &#x20;It pledges to support the environment with tree planting, recycling and fewer single use plastics.

  [^cite_note-BBCrun-320]

  [^cite_note-BBCrunS-323]

  [^cite_note-LGCrun-324]

- Eradicating waiting lists within two years by giving the NHS an extra £17bn a year and increasing the use of the private sector in the NHS, giving tax breaks to nurses and doctors to increase their number, and other measures including less tax for private healthcare and insurance, offering vouchers for private healthcare and looking to France's insurance-based health model.

  [^cite_note-BBCrun-320]

  [^cite_note-ITVrun-319]

  [^cite_note-Sky_News,_Reform_UK_policies-321]

- Increasing the number of police officers by 40,000 in five years, "clamp down on all crime and antisocial behaviour", by instituting [zero tolerance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zero_tolerance) policing.

  [^cite_note-STDrun-325]

- Introducing a "patriotic curriculum" in schools, such that, for example, where imperialism or slavery is covered, examples are also given of non-European instances. "[Transgender ideology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transgender_ideology)" would be banned, no gender questioning, social transitioning or "pronoun swapping" would be allowed in schools, universities would have to offer two-year courses to reduce student debt.

  [^cite_note-ITVrun-319]

  [^cite_note-BBCrun-320]

  &#x20;Scrap interest on [student loans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Student_loan) and extend the loan capital repayment periods to 45 years.

  [^cite_note-326]

  &#x20;Encouraging the use of private schools via a 20% tax relief on private schooling.

  [^cite_note-327]

- Increasing defence spending to 2.5% of GDP in three years, and then to 3% over the following three. 30,000 additional people would be [recruited to join the army](3135672.md).

  [^cite_note-STDrun-325]

- Focus on new rail and road infrastructure in coastal regions, Wales, the North and the Midlands.

  [^cite_note-328]

  [^cite_note-329]

  &#x20;[Public utilities](171136.md) and critical infrastructure would come under 50% [public ownership](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_ownership), the other 50% being owned by UK [pension fund](43615.md).

  [^cite_note-LGCrun-324]

  [^cite_note-STDrun-325]

  [^cite_note-330]

- Increasing the farming budget to £3bn, focus on small farms, bring young people into farming.

  [^cite_note-331]

- Stopping EU fleets taking British fishing quotas, ban massive supertrawlers, and other fisheries measures.

  [^cite_note-332]

- Replacing the existing second chamber, the [House of Lords](13658.md), with a more democratic smaller alternative, having a referendum on the replacement of [first-past-the-post voting](261709.md) with a system of [proportional representation](23298.md).

  [^cite_note-333]

- Eliminating the [TV licence fee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Television_licensing_in_the_United_Kingdom).

  [^cite_note-BBCrun-320]

- Leaving the [European Convention on Human Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Convention_on_Human_Rights).

  [^cite_note-Sky_News,_Reform_UK_policies-321]

- Immediately cutting the rate of corporation tax from 25% to 20% and then further reduce corporation tax to 15% in the third year of parliament.

  [^cite_note-IFS-334]

- £150 billion per year in spending reductions, including public services and working-age benefits.

  [^cite_note-IFS-334]

Reform UK said that the total cost of its manifesto would be £140 billion but say that they would raise £150 billion. According to Reform UK, this money would be raised from the scrapping of net zero subsidies, the ending of payments of interest on [quantitative easing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quantitative_easing) reserves to banks, the halving of foreign aid, cuts to working age benefits and other public spending reductions.[^cite_note-IFS-334][^cite_note-Daily_Telegraph,_Reform_UK_policies-322][^cite_note-Sky_News,_June_2024-318] The party said that it would "cut bureaucracy \[…] without touching frontline services",[^cite_note-LGCrun-324] while the [Institute for Fiscal Studies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Institute_for_Fiscal_Studies) said that the savings required "would almost certainly require substantial cuts to the quantity or quality of public services" and that the sums of the costs of tax cuts and spending increases and savings proposed "do not add up" and were based on "extremely optimistic assumptions".[^cite_note-335][^cite_note-336][^cite_note-IFS-334]

The party's manifesto was criticised in a 2024 article by John Pring as threatening the rights of disabled people and as posing significant safeguarding risks to benefit claimants.[^cite_note-337]

Analysis in 2024 found that Reform UK's tax plans disproportionately benefit high earners.[^cite_note-Sky_News,_June_2024-318] Farage said that his plans to raise the minimum threshold at which workers start paying tax from £12,571 to £20,000, would result in millions of low-paid workers not paying tax altogether.[^cite_note-Sky_News,_June_2024-318] Reform UK wants to raise the higher rate threshold of tax from £50,271 to £70,000, which would result in a tax cut of close to £6,000 for the top 10% of earners. According to analysis for [Sky News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sky_News) this would far outweigh any benefit to the lowest earners.[^cite_note-Sky_News,_June_2024-318]

### 2025

In April 2025, Reform called for the [nationalisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nationalisation) of the steel plant in Scunthorpe and government take-over of two electric arc furnaces at Liberty Steel's plant in Rotherham.[^cite_note-338]

In September 2025, Reform said they would abolish [indefinite leave to remain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indefinite_leave_to_remain).[^cite_note-339] Reform also proposed significant reforms to disability benefits, primarily focused on reducing the welfare bill by tightening eligibility and reassessment processes, particularly for those with less severe or non-serious conditions.[^cite_note-Pring,_September_2025-340] The party aims to move over a million people back into work, suggesting that for those considered fit to work, benefits would be withdrawn if they decline two job offers or fail to find employment within four months. They argue that work itself can be beneficial for mental health.[^cite_note-341]

Reform UK has echoed Trump's "Make America Great Again" slogan, using "make Britain great again", and has set up its own [Department of Government Efficiency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Department_of_Government_Efficiency) (DOGE), mirroring the initiative by the second Trump administration to cut government spending, which as of 25 March 2025 was led by Elon Musk.[^cite_note-342]

Policies on immigration and asylum include: leaving the European Convention on Human Rights (ECHR); repealing the Human Rights Act 1998 and replacing it with a "British Bill of Rights"; disapplying the 1951 Refugee Convention, the UN Convention Against Torture, and the Council of Europe Anti-Trafficking Convention (ECAT); creating detention powers without Hardial Singh constraints; passing an illegal migration (mass deportation bill) and deny asylum for illegal immigrants.[^cite_note-343] Reform said in November it would remove the rights of EU citizens to claim benefits, which would save £6.4 billion for 2025 rising to £10 billion by the end of the decade.[^cite_note-344]

### Climate change

Further information: [Climate change in the United Kingdom](25369297.md)

A November 2024 poll conducted by [YouGov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/YouGov) found that Reform UK voters are twice as likely as the general public to believe that climate change is not caused by human activity.[^cite_note-345]

A policy document named "Reform is Essential" published in late 2022 contained a subchapter named "*An honest debate on climate change*" in which it said among other things: "Reform UK fully accepts that climate change is real, after all it has happened for millions of years based on multiple factors completely outside human control or influence. Warming has of course taken place over the last approximately 150 years, with signs over recent years that it is now leveling off."[^cite_note-346] In July 2024, in the run-up to the general election, the party broadly opposed the UK's legally binding net‑zero commitments, repeatedly pledging to scrap them, and according to *[The Guardian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Guardian)* many of its leading members had downplayed or denied climate science consensus.[^cite_note-347] This is a marked change in British politics which up until recently enjoyed cross-party consensus on achieving net zero.[^cite_note-348][^cite_note-349][^cite_note-350] Before the 2024 election and following it, the party attracted several donors associated with the [fossil fuel industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fossil_fuel_industry) or [climate scepticism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_scepticism), including David Lilley, Roger Nagioff and Fiona Cottrell.[^cite_note-i366-351][^cite_note-t870-352]

In an interview with the BBC in October 2021, then party chairman, Richard Tice criticised the Conservative Party's plans to decarbonise the economy, saying: "It is not net zero – it is literally net stupid." Instead, he suggested that the UK should focus on exploiting reserves of [shale gas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shale_gas).[^cite_note-bbc-tice-attacks-316] Tice repeated the same stance in April 2022 saying that the Conservative Party's climate policy was out of touch with their voters and that Reform wanted to "cut taxes, go for growth, become self-reliant and use our own shale gas."[^cite_note-tele-new-brexit-353] Reform UK's 2024 election manifesto rejected net zero and encouraged the use of fossil fuels.[^cite_note-Daily_Telegraph,_Reform_UK_policies-322]

In June 2024, Pallavi Sethi and [Bob Ward](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bob_Ward_\(communications_director\)) of the [Grantham Research Institute on Climate Change and the Environment (London School of Economics)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grantham_Research_Institute_on_Climate_Change_and_the_Environment_\(London_School_of_Economics\)) at [London School of Economics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/London_School_of_Economics) accused Reform UK and its leaders Nigel Farage and Richard Tice of promoting misinformation about climate change.[^cite_note-354]

*The Guardian* reported in June 2024 that climate change deniers and sceptics were donating to Reform and Nigel Farage in 2024. The donors with stakes in the fossil fuel industry who were said to have donated were [Terence Mordaunt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terence_Mordaunt) and [Jeremy Hosking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeremy_Hosking). Donors who had donated to both [The Global Warming Policy Foundation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Global_Warming_Policy_Foundation) (GWPF) and Reform were [Jon Moynihan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jon_Moynihan), [Nigel Vinson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nigel_Vinson), and [Michael Hintze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Hintze).[^cite_note-t870-352]

In February 2025, Tice said: "There's no evidence that man-made CO2 is going to change climate change. Given that it's gone on for millions of years, it will go on for millions of years" and that there were "a thousand" scientists who agreed with him, which was "not a minority". Scientists told *Sky News* that his first point was "categorically wrong", "missing the point" and that the "evidence is clear", regarding the second point, 99% of scientific publications agree with the scientific consensus.[^cite_note-355]

In March 2025, *[The New York Times](30680.md)* reported: "Most of those bankrolling Reform in 2024 were multimillionaires, individuals and companies based in low-tax offshore countries, climate change sceptics, or those with investments in fossil fuels or other climate-polluting industries."[^cite_note-356]

In June 2024, Reform UK had accepted "more than £2.3 million from oil and gas interests, highly polluting industries, and 'climate science deniers' since December 2019".[^cite_note-357][^cite_note-358]

In April 2025, Farage said that the current government's net zero policy was "lunacy" and that "this could be the next Brexit", "where Parliament is so hopelessly out of touch with the country.” In response, the [Secretary of State for Energy Security and Net Zero](72970227.md) [Ed Miliband](1290018.md) accused him of peddling "nonsense and lies".[^cite_note-359][^cite_note-360] In July 2025, Reform UK's [Mayor of Greater Lincolnshire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mayor_of_Greater_Lincolnshire), Andrea Jenkyns, said, "Do I believe that climate change exists? No."[^cite_note-361]

In July 2025, Richard Tice, Dame Andrea Jenkyns and Reform UK councillor Sean Matthews, who leads [Lincolnshire County Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lincolnshire_County_Council), launched a campaign opposing wind and solar farms and battery storage facilities, with Tice saying the campaign would use "every lever available" to stop these developments.[^cite_note-362]

In November 2025, when asked by [Sky News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sky_News) if humans have impacted the climate, Tice said: "Possibly, but if so, a very modest percentage." He accepted the need to update infrastructure in Britain to cope with a changing climate. When asked whether he accepted that the climate was warming at an unprecedented rate, Tice said: "From the data that I've seen, from previous ice core data, I think the answer to that is questionable." [NASA](18426568.md) has stated "human activity is the principal cause" of unprecedentedly fast warming and 234 UN scientists from the [Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change](15030.md) have said it is "unequivocal" that humans have caused "widespread and rapid changes".[^cite_note-363]

### 2026

In February 2026, Robert Jenrick said that Reform would reinstate the [two child benefit cap](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benefit_cap),[^cite_note-364] as part of an attempt to be more "fiscally responsible" in their policies.[^cite_note-\:6-365] This came four months after [Rachel Reeves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rachel_Reeves) announced that the benefit cap was to be abolished,[^cite_note-366] and was a change of policy, it previously having been to remove it.[^cite_note-\:6-365][^cite_note-367]

In 2026, Reform published draft legislation, *Cryptoassets and Digital Finance Bill*,[^cite_note-reformparty/crypto-bill-368] since removed from the Reform UK website.[^cite_note-bbc/cg4w6wqye32o-369][^cite_note-bbc/cg4vnd0d17ro-370][^cite_note-standard/b1230382-371]

In August 2026 [Richard Tice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Tice) said: "Climate change has always happened. It always will. Maybe the smarter thing to do is to adapt to it than think arrogantly that you can stop it. When it's a bit warm, let's enjoy it. And if it means that English wine and sparkling wine gets better and better, fantastic."[^cite_note-372][^cite_note-373]

Also in August 2026, Reform UK proposed cutting £50 billion in welfare/benefits if it were in government.[^cite_note-50bn-374] It estimates 2.89 million people would have their disability payments' removed, or altered under plans to abolish [Personal Independence Payments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Personal_Independence_Payment) (Pip) for working age people. Reform UK proposed making companies pay for any employees off sick for the first two years.[^cite_note-50bn-374] A new system, known as a Health Security Allowance, would replace both Pip and the [Universal Credit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_Credit) health element.[^cite_note-50bn-374]

### Foreign policy

Farage supported Israeli airstrikes against Iran during the [Twelve-Day War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twelve-Day_War) in 2025, arguing Israel had "no choice" in the situation.[^cite_note-375][^cite_note-\:2-376] In June 2025, Farage said he was opposed to "de-escalation" with Iran and instead wanted to see its "bloody awful regime" be "wiped out".[^cite_note-\:2-376]

Farage initially supported Israel and the US in the [2026 Iran war](82533582.md). He criticised the decision by the prime minister, Keir Starmer, for the UK to [not be involved in offensive strikes against Iran and to deny the USA access](77280609.md#Iran) to UK military bases to enable them. In response, Farage said "The Prime Minister needs to change his mind on the use of our military bases and back the Americans in this vital fight against Iran!"[^cite_note-377][^cite_note-378][^cite_note-379] He also stated his support for the US achieving [regime change](154074.md) in Iran,[^cite_note-380] one of their stated objectives for the conflict.[^cite_note-381][^cite_note-382] However, shortly after, Farage reversed his position to say Britain should not get "involved in another foreign war",[^cite_note-383] while Jenrick said that Reform's position was that "this war needs to come to an end as soon as possible because it is making Britain poorer", criticising the "liberal interventionists of the early 21st century".[^cite_note-384]

## Voting history

In October 2024, all Reform UK MPs voted against the [Employment Rights Bill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Employment_Rights_Act_2025).[^cite_note-388] A policy within the bill is workers' prevention from harassment, which has been heavily criticised by Farage and other Reform UK politicians, who have referred to it as a "banter ban".[^cite_note-ERB-389] The general secretary of the [Trades Union Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trades_Union_Congress) (TUC), [Paul Nowak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Nowak_\(trade_unionist\)), said on 28 April 2025, "The likes of Reform are defying their supporters by voting against improvements to workers' rights at every stage."[^cite_note-ERB-389]

In January 2025, all Reform UK MPs voted for an amendment to the [Children's Wellbeing and Schools Bill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Children's_Wellbeing_and_Schools_Bill) regarding a new national inquiry into grooming gangs. The amendment was intended to block the bill and its passing would have halted the bill's progress in Parliament.[^cite_note-390] The Children's Wellbeing and Schools Bill aims to improve laws regarding home-schooling and support for children in care, improve school inspections and improve safeguards regarding private education facilities.[^cite_note-391] The amendment to the bill was lost by 364 votes to 111, a majority of 253 against the amendment.[^cite_note-397] Farage stated Reform UK will launch their own independent inquiry and pay for it themselves as the government one was rejected in order to respond to the "overwhelming demand" of the public to know the "full, unvarnished truth". Farage said the attacks were racist against white children by Pakistani rapists. Farage also criticised the Conservatives saying, "Talk is cheap. The Conservatives had 14 years in government to launch an inquiry. The establishment has failed the victims of grooming gangs on every level."[^cite_note-398][^cite_note-399]

Of Reform UK's 8 MPs, 5 voted against the [Terminally Ill Adults (End of Life) Bill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terminally_Ill_Adults_\(End_of_Life\)_Bill) and the other 3 did not vote.[^cite_note-400]

## Funding and structure

Further information: [Reform UK financial allegations](83649192.md)

In its early days, the Brexit Party officially had three members, who were Farage, Tracey Knowles and Mehrtash A'Zami. The party opted for signing up registered supporters rather than members. The party structure was criticised for not providing the party's over 115,000 paying registered supporters[^cite_note-401] with any voting power to influence party policy;[^cite_note-business-structure-306] Farage retained a high level of control over decision-making, including hand-picking candidates himself.[^cite_note-business-structure-306][^cite_note-402] Since 2021, the party has options to become a member, rather than a supporter.[^cite_note-403]

Initially the Reform UK party was a limited company (the Reform UK Party Limited)[^cite_note-INDY-404] with fifteen [shares](867877.md). Farage owned 53% of the shares in the company, giving him a controlling majority. The other shareholders were Tice, who holds about a third, and Chief Executive Paul Oakden and Party Treasurer Mehrtash A'Zami who each held less than seven percent.[^cite_note-INDY-404] In August 2024 Paul Oakden was removed and Farage took over his shares, giving him 60% ownership.[^cite_note-Walker-405] As of 2025, ownership of the party was transferred from Farage to a new business legally constituted as Reform 2025 Limited, a [company limited by guarantee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Company_limited_by_guarantee), replacing the original company which was controlled by Farage as majority shareholder.[^cite_note-NF-406][^cite_note-DF-407] The directors and guarantors of the new company are Farage and [Zia Yusuf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zia_Yusuf), who will effectively control the new company.[^cite_note-NF-406][^cite_note-DF-407][^cite_note-408] The business's filing stated that it had no "person with significant control".[^cite_note-NF-406]

In 2019 Farage said the party would largely be funded by small donations and that they raised "£750,000 in donations online, all in small sums of less than £500" in their first ten days. The party also accepts large donations.[^cite_note-Tele001-409] He further said that the party would not be taking money from the key former UKIP funder [Arron Banks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arron_Banks).[^cite_note-guardian-19][^cite_note-guardian2-410] Farage personally faced questions during the [2019 electoral campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_European_Parliament_election_in_the_United_Kingdom) after *[Channel 4 News](273621.md)* revealed [undeclared travel and accommodation benefits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nigel_Farage#Arron_Banks_funding) provided by Banks before Farage joined the Brexit Party, and on 21 May 2019 the European Parliament formally opened an investigation.[^cite_note-411] In response to the reporting, the Brexit Party banned *Channel 4 News* from its events.[^cite_note-412]

In 2019, £6.4m was donated to the party by [Christopher Harborne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christopher_Harborne),[^cite_note-eye1517-413] and £200,000 by [Jeremy Hosking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeremy_Hosking), a former donor to the [Conservative Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_Party_\(UK\)).[^cite_note-Tele001-409] 2023 donations included £200,000 from [Terence Mordaunt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terence_Mordaunt)'s company First Corporate Consultants Ltd.[^cite_note-Allegretti_Willoughby_2024_i732-414]

Two days before the [2019 European election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_European_Parliament_election_in_the_United_Kingdom), Farage accused the [Electoral Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electoral_Commission_\(United_Kingdom\)) of "interfering in the electoral process" after the independent watchdog visited the Brexit Party headquarters for "active oversight and regulation" of party funding.[^cite_note-415] Official donations of £500 or more must be given by a "permissible donor", who should either be somebody listed on the British electoral roll or a business registered at [Companies House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Companies_House) and operating in Britain. When asked if the party took donations in foreign currency, Farage replied: "Absolutely not, we only take sterling – end of conversation." [Shadow Chancellor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shadow_Chancellor_of_the_Exchequer) [John McDonnell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_McDonnell) called for "a full and open and transparent, independent inquiry into the funding of Mr Farage".[^cite_note-416] The Electoral Commission reported in July 2019 that following its visit it made recommendations to the party for more robust internal controls on permissible donations, as those in place had not been adequate, and that the party had returned a donation of £1,000 whose source could not be identified as acceptable.[^cite_note-417]

In May 2024, *[The Guardian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Guardian)* said that 80% of the party's funding, in loans and donations, came from Tice. It reported Tice as saying that the Conservatives spend £35 million annually, while Reform spends less than £1.5 million.[^cite_note-418]

On 12 June 2025, [openDemocracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/OpenDemocracy) reported that "Reform has received almost £5m from wealthy donors since 2023, including those with links to fossil fuels, the financial services industry and tax havens".[^cite_note-openDem-419] Around a quarter of donations have been from former Conservative Party donors and Reform UK has an unusually high number of overseas donors with connections to tax havens.[^cite_note-openDem-419]

In the first three months of 2026, [cryptocurrency](36662188.md) tycoons, made several large donations to Reform UK: Harborne donated a further £3 million, and [Ben Delo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ben_Delo), billionaire co-founder of [BitMEX](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BitMEX), donated £4 million.[^cite_note-Independent1-420]

### Delo and Harborne record donations

On 11 September 2026, after allegations of illegal funding for Reform UK, Ben Delo donated a further £36 million, the largest donation to a political party in British history to that date, beating the previous record of £10 million to the Conservatives in 2023.[^cite_note-\:22-421][^cite_note-422][^cite_note-423] The following day, Christopher Harborne matched Delo's £36 million donation. The £72 million in donations exceeded total donations to all UK political parties combined in 2025,[^cite_note-424] and was far in excess of the roughly £25 million given to other parties in 2026 so far,[^cite_note-425] and the £43 million that Labour has received from trade unions since 2020.[^cite_note-426] Delo said that he made the donation because he wanted a "fair fight and a level playing field".[^cite_note-\:22-421]

The size of the donations led to calls from the [Liberal Democrats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberal_Democrats_\(UK\)), the [Green Party](79787.md), and [Unite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unite_the_Union) general secretary [Sharon Graham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sharon_Graham) for a cap on political donations by a single donor.[^cite_note-\:111-427] Some MPs, including [Stella Creasy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stella_Creasy), had made proposals for such changes earlier in the month.[^cite_note-428][^cite_note-429] [Conservative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_Party_\(UK\)) shadow foreign secretary [Tom Tugendhat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tom_Tugendhat) warned of an "Americanisation of politics where money just sort of runs free".[^cite_note-\:111-427] Liberal Democrat spokesperson [Lisa Smart](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lisa_Smart) accused Nigel Farage of "buying his way into power".[^cite_note-\:22-421] [Trades Union Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trades_Union_Congress) general secretary [Paul Nowak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Nowak_\(trade_unionist\)) said the government must "stop the super-rich corrupting our politics".[^cite_note-\:111-427][^cite_note-430] In a 14 September [YouGov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/YouGov) poll, 60% of respondents supported a cap on donations from individuals and entities, and 22% were opposed.[^cite_note-431]

Delo and Harborne had both lived outside the UK until earlier in 2026.[^cite_note-\:111-427] In March 2026, the government had announced legislation to cap donations from foreign residents at £100,000 per year, including for the first year after their return. The legislation was still in Parliament as of September 2026, but is due to apply retroactively to all donations made since its announcement.[^cite_note-\:111-427][^cite_note-\:32-432] Delo had said in April that he would return to the UK, and Harborne is believed to have registered his residence in June.[^cite_note-\:32-432][^cite_note-\:43-433] Nigel Farage said the donations were "compliant with the law today", but declined to say whether they would be permitted under the proposed changes.[^cite_note-434][^cite_note-\:43-433]

### Membership

In June 2019, the Brexit Party reportedly had 115,000 paying supporters, though the party did not have an official membership.[^cite_note-435]

During the week after the 3 June 2024 announcement of Farage's resumption of party leadership, *ITV News* reported that party membership increased by 50% to 45,000.[^cite_note-436] By September of that year, the party said it had 80,000 members,[^cite_note-437] and they hit 100,000 by the end of November.[^cite_note-438] The party launched an online tracker on 23 December 2024 as it approached overtaking the number of Conservative members; it had 120,549 at noon of that day.[^cite_note-439] On 26 December, the party said it had surpassed the Conservative party's most recently published total of 131,680, making it the second-largest party by membership.[^cite_note-Ref/Con_membership_National-83] On 10 February, the party hit 200,000 members.[^cite_note-440] During the fallout from the dispute around Rupert Lowe in March, those around Lowe claimed that as many as 7,000 members had resigned in protest, though Reform denied this.[^cite_note-441] Following an incline to over 230,000 members up to May,[^cite_note-442] the membership figures have stagnated and dropped slightly since.[^cite_note-443][^cite_note-Burqa_membership_drop-444]

As of January 2025, the party has 7,800 members in Wales.[^cite_note-445] In June 2025, the party said it had over 15,000 members in London[^cite_note-446] and 11,000 members in Scotland.[^cite_note-447] In Scotland, the party claims to be the third-biggest, beating the Scottish Conservative total of 6,941 in January 2025.[^cite_note-448]

According to research by [Tim Bale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tim_Bale) and the *Party Members Project* done just after the 2024 election, Reform members are similar to those of other parties.[^cite_note-Bale-449] The average age is 61 with very few between 18–24 and almost half of its members are over 65.[^cite_note-Bale-449] Members are predominantly from the middle class, and has a substantial share of its membership in the [Midlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Midlands) and [North](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_England).[^cite_note-Bale-449] Nearly all voted leave, and 90% of them consider themselves as being some level of [right-wing](56522.md).[^cite_note-Bale-449]

As of 10 June 2025, the membership had dropped from 237,099 to 234,175 in a week. This followed newly elected Reform UK MP [Sarah Pochin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarah_Pochin)'s Commons maiden speech in which she asked if the UK should ban the burqa for public safety as France, Denmark and Belgium had done.[^cite_note-Burqa_membership_drop-444]

As of 29 August 2025, the party had 237,000 members and 450 branches, up from 80,000 members and no branches at the same time in the previous year.[^cite_note-450]

As of 12 December 2025, Reform UK said it had over 268,000 members, while according to *[The Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Times)* the Labour Party's membership had fallen to below 250,000. At the time, standard annual membership of Reform cost £25 compared to £70.50 for Labour.[^cite_note-451]

The six members joining the party leader and chairman to form the inaugural Reform UK Board were announced on 22 August 2025.[^cite_note-452]

## Leadership

### Leaders

Main article: [Leader of Reform UK](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leader_of_Reform_UK)

Reform UK has had three leaders. [Catherine Blaiklock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catherine_Blaiklock) was its first leader, in early 2019.[^cite_note-guard-453] [Nigel Farage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nigel_Farage) was leader from March 2019 until March 2021, when he resigned and [Richard Tice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Tice) took on the role.[^cite_note-454] On 3 June 2024 it was announced that Tice had invited Farage to return as leader, an offer Farage accepted.

## Election results

Main articles: [Brexit Party election results](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brexit_Party_election_results) and [Reform UK election results](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reform_UK_election_results)

### 2019 European Parliament election as the Brexit Party

Main article: [2019 European Parliament election in the United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_European_Parliament_election_in_the_United_Kingdom)

The Brexit Party stood candidates in Great Britain at the [2019 European Parliament election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_European_Parliament_election), including the former Conservative Party [Minister of State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_of_State), [Ann Widdecombe](2422.md),[^cite_note-457] the journalist, [Annunziata Rees-Mogg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Annunziata_Rees-Mogg) (a former Conservative general election candidate and the sister of the Conservative MP and Brexit advocate, [Jacob Rees-Mogg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacob_Rees-Mogg)), the [Leave Means Leave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leave_Means_Leave) co-founder, [Richard Tice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Tice),[^cite_note-guardian2-410] the writers, [Claire Fox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Claire_Fox) and [James Heartfield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Heartfield) (both once part of the [Revolutionary Communist Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revolutionary_Communist_Party_\(UK,_1978\)) and later writers for *[Spiked](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spiked_\(magazine\))*),[^cite_note-458][^cite_note-459] [Stuart Waiton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stuart_Waiton) (a fellow *Spiked* contributor)[^cite_note-460][^cite_note-461] [James Glancy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Glancy), a former member of the [Royal Marines](93985.md) and the [Special Boat Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_Boat_Service) who was awarded the [Conspicuous Gallantry Cross](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspicuous_Gallantry_Cross),[^cite_note-462] [Martin Daubney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martin_Daubney), a journalist and commentator,[^cite_note-463] [David Bull](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Bull_\(politician\)), author and television presenter,[^cite_note-464][^cite_note-465] [Brian Monteith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brian_Monteith), a former Conservative Party [MSP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Member_of_the_Scottish_Parliament), [Rupert Lowe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rupert_Lowe), a businessman[^cite_note-466] and retired Rear Admiral [Roger Lane-Nott](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roger_Lane-Nott).[^cite_note-467] [John Longworth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Longworth_\(businessman\)), the former director-general of the British Chambers of Commerce, announced he would be standing as a candidate for the party on 15 April 2019.[^cite_note-468] The party was not registered in Northern Ireland and did not field candidates there.[^cite_note-469]

A survey of 781 Conservative Party councillors found that 40% planned to vote for the Brexit Party.[^cite_note-470] On 17 April 2019, the former Labour and [Respect Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Respect_Party) MP [George Galloway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Galloway) announced his support for the Brexit Party "for one-time only" in the 2019 European Parliament election.[^cite_note-471] On 24 April, the political columnist [Tim Montgomerie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tim_Montgomerie) announced that he would vote for the party and endorsed Widdecombe's candidature,[^cite_note-472] and the Conservative MP [Lucy Allan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lucy_Allan_\(politician\)) described the candidates of the party as "fantastic".[^cite_note-473] On 2 May, one of the party's candidates for the North West constituency, Sally Bate, resigned from the party in response to previous comments made by Claire Fox, the lead candidate in the constituency, on the [Warrington bombings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warrington_bombings).[^cite_note-474]

In May 2019, several polls forecast the party polling first for the European elections,[^cite_note-475] though earlier polls had suggested it would come third to Labour and the Conservatives.[^cite_note-476] The party held 14 seats, acquired through defections, going into the elections, and saw an increase of 15. It won five more seats than UKIP, had at the [previous election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_European_Parliament_election_in_the_United_Kingdom), under Farage's leadership.

#### Results

The party won 29 seats in the [election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_European_Parliament_election_in_the_United_Kingdom), becoming the biggest single party in the [Ninth European Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ninth_European_Parliament). The [CDU/CSU Union](10796483.md) also won 29 seats in [Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_European_Parliament_election_in_Germany), but it was an alliance and not a party. Three of the 29 resigned the whip in December 2019 to support the [Conservative Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_Party_\(UK\)) at the [2019 general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_United_Kingdom_general_election), while a fourth, [John Longworth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Longworth_\(businessman\)), was expelled for "repeatedly undermining" the party's election strategy.[^cite_note-477]

### 2019 general election

Main article: [2019 United Kingdom general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_United_Kingdom_general_election)

On 19 April, Farage said that the party intended to stand candidates at the [2019 general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_United_Kingdom_general_election),[^cite_note-478] but would not stand candidates against the 28 Eurosceptic Conservative MPs who opposed the [Brexit withdrawal agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brexit_withdrawal_agreement).[^cite_note-479] In the [Peterborough by-election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Peterborough_by-election) in June, the Brexit Party came second with 28% of the vote, 7% ahead of the Conservatives and 2% behind Labour.

Following [Boris Johnson](19065069.md)'s election as Prime Minister, Farage unveiled the names of 635 general election candidates for the Brexit Party, including himself.[^cite_note-480] On 8 September 2019, Farage wrote an article in the *[The Sunday Telegraph](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Sunday_Telegraph)* and the party took out advertisements in Sunday newspapers offering an electoral pact with the Conservative Party in the forthcoming general election, whereby the Brexit Party would not be opposed by the Conservatives in traditional Labour Party seats in the north of England, the Midlands and Wales, and the Brexit Party would not contest seats in which they could split the Leave vote. Farage wrote that Boris Johnson should ask himself "does he want to sign a non-aggression pact with me and return to Downing Street?"[^cite_note-481]



Constituencies, highlighted, which the Brexit Party contested at the [2019 general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_United_Kingdom_general_election)

Farage's proposition was rejected by Johnson.[^cite_note-482] On 11 November, Farage said that his party would not stand in any of the 317 seats won by the Conservatives at the last election. Conservative Party chairman [James Cleverly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Cleverly) welcomed this, although he stated that the parties had not been in contact.[^cite_note-483] *[Newsnight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newsnight)* reported that conversations between members of the Brexit Party and the pro-Brexit Conservative group, the [European Research Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Research_Group) (ERG) had led to this decision.[^cite_note-newsnight11-484]

The Brexit Party is reported to have requested that Boris Johnson publicly state he would not extend the Brexit transition period beyond the planned date of 31 December 2020 and that he wished for a Canada-style free-trade agreement with the EU. Johnson did make a statement covering these two issues, something which Farage referenced as key when announcing he was standing down some candidates, but both the Brexit Party and the Conservatives denied that any deal was done between them.[^cite_note-newsnight11-484][^cite_note-485][^cite_note-486] The decision to not run in those seats met with criticism by some Brexit Party supporters and candidates, and some candidates who had been selected to run for [Conservative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_Party_\(UK\)) seats opted to run as [independent candidates](2172378.md) on a [Pro-Brexit](41688778.md) platform.[^cite_note-487]

#### Results

The party failed to win any seats in the general election.[^cite_note-killed-26] Its best second places were in [Barnsley Central](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barnsley_Central), where Victoria Felton won 30.4% of the vote,[^cite_note-488] and [Barnsley East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barnsley_East), where Jim Ferguson won 29.2%.[^cite_note-489] High third places were in [Hartlepool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hartlepool_\(constituency\)), where Richard Tice won 25.8% of the vote,[^cite_note-hull-490] and [Kingston upon Hull West and Hessle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingston_upon_Hull_West_and_Hessle), where [Michelle Dewberry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michelle_Dewberry) won 18%.[^cite_note-hull-490]

### 2024 general election

Main article: [2024 United Kingdom general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_United_Kingdom_general_election)

On 22 May 2024, Prime Minister [Rishi Sunak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rishi_Sunak) announced the date of the [general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_United_Kingdom_general_election) as 4 July. The next day, Tice launched the Reform UK campaign, promising to field candidates in 630 seats including himself in [Boston and Skegness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boston_and_Skegness).[^cite_note-491] He said the party wanted to make this the "immigration election".[^cite_note-bbc-rules-out-49] Farage initially ruled out standing, saying it was "not the right time" but promised to "do my bit to help".[^cite_note-guardian-u-turn-61] In the first week of the campaign, Reform's predicted vote in opinion polls rose from 11% to 13%, although many commentators predicted their share would be squeezed[^cite_note-492] and the Conservatives announced policies targeted at Reform voters, such as [national service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_service).[^cite_note-493]

On 3 June, Farage became the leader of Reform UK, and pollsters reported an increase in support for the party, in two cases polling within 2% of the Conservative Party.[^cite_note-494] BBC analyst Peter Barnes commented on 9 June that the change in leadership "has clearly had a positive impact on the party's performance in the polls", and that this "has come at the expense of the Conservatives".[^cite_note-495] A poll conducted after the BBC's seven-party debate held on 7 June found Farage to be the winner with 25% support, his closest rival being Labour Party deputy leader [Angela Rayner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angela_Rayner), on 19%. The debate majored on the [Normandy landings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Normandy_landings), war veterans, immigration and the [National Health Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Health_Service) (NHS).[^cite_note-496]

Farage said that his aim was to make Reform the [Official Opposition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/His_Majesty's_Most_Loyal_Opposition) party in Parliament.[^cite_note-497] Reform would be standing in 609 out of 650 constituencies.[^cite_note-498] As part of an electoral pact with the [Social Democratic Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_Democratic_Party_\(UK,_1990%E2%80%93present\)), the two parties stood aside from each other in six constituencies and a dozen candidates stood under a joint Reform-SDP banner.[^cite_note-SDP_Pact-499]

On 10 June, the Reform UK candidate for [Bexhill and Battle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bexhill_and_Battle), Ian Gribbin, was reported as having said in 2022 that: "Britain would be in a far better state today had we taken Hitler up on his offer of neutrality." Gribbin apologised without reservation for the comment and upset caused.[^cite_note-500] A party spokesman defended Gribbin by saying that "his historical perspective of what the UK could have done in the 30s was shared by the vast majority of the British establishment including the BBC of its day, and is probably true" that the comments made by Gribbin were not endorsements of the stances and that the party would continue to support him.[^cite_note-501][^cite_note-502] *The Times* reported on 13 June that 41 of Reform candidates were [Facebook](7529378.md) friends with the British [neo-fascist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neo-fascism) leader [Gary Raikes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gary_Raikes).[^cite_note-503] After revelations about the candidates,[^cite_note-tns001-504] Farage said on 18 June that the party had hired a vetting company, but had been "stitched up" by them. The company, vetting.com, responded that there had been insufficient time to complete their work, the election having been called earlier than expected.[^cite_note-tns001-504]

In the campaign, the party used the slogan "Britain Needs Reform".[^cite_note-505] Its party election video, broadcast nationally on 13 June, showed silently and continuously for 4 minutes and 40 seconds the six words "Britain is Broken. Britain Needs Reform".[^cite_note-506] On 13 June, [YouGov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/YouGov) polling put Reform at 19% and the Conservatives 18%. Farage declared "We are now the opposition to Labour."[^cite_note-507] On 15 June, the BBC's [Laura Kuenssberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laura_Kuenssberg) said that "the most optimistic Reform politicians can't name more than five or six seats where they reckon they could win."[^cite_note-508] Pollsters Survation published the results of a survey of 42,269 voters employing [multilevel regression with poststratification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multilevel_regression_with_poststratification) (MRP) which predicted that Reform would win seven seats and YouGov's MRP survey predicted five seats.[^cite_note-PoliticoYou-509]

On 20 June, the BBC reported that while Farage has been criticised by some Muslim organisations for saying that a growing number of young Muslims do not subscribe to British values, Muslim entrepreneur [Zia Yusuf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zia_Yusuf) had just given the party a donation amounting to hundreds of thousands of pounds and said that the country has lost control of its borders. He said that it was his "patriotic duty" to fund Farage and Reform UK.[^cite_note-510]

Farage was criticised during the campaign for suggesting that the West had provoked Russia's invasion of Ukraine by expanding the EU and [NATO](21133.md) military alliance eastwards. Farage also said that "of course" the war was the fault of Putin.[^cite_note-511]

On 27 June, *[Channel 4 News](273621.md)* revealed homophobic, racist and Islamophobic comments made by party campaigners in [Clacton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clacton_\(constituency\)),[^cite_note-512][^cite_note-BBC_News-513] including an individual calling Sunak a "[Paki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paki)" (a racist slur against those of South Asian heritage),[^cite_note-smh-514] and suggesting the army should shoot at [boats bringing illegal migrants](59538230.md) to the UK,[^cite_note-BBC_News-513] and another campaigner calling the [LGBT flag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBT_flag) "degenerate". Sunak responded that hearing the slur against him "hurts and it makes me angry".[^cite_note-AP-racist-515][^cite_note-nyt-516][^cite_note-517] Farage described the anti-gay comments as "vulgar, drunk and wrong"[^cite_note-518] and condemned the racist comments, before suggesting the programme was a "set up" by Channel 4, as the individual who made the racist slur against Sunak, Andrew Parker, was an actor and that it alluded to foul play.[^cite_note-AP-racist-515] The party said it made a complaint against Channel 4 for "electoral interference",[^cite_note-519] although reports on 28 June suggested the [Electoral Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electoral_Commission_\(United_Kingdom\)) had not received such a complaint.[^cite_note-lbc-520] Channel 4 commented: "We met Mr Parker for the first time at Reform UK party headquarters, where he was a Reform party canvasser. We did not pay the Reform UK canvasser or anyone else in this report. Mr Parker was not known to Channel 4 News and was filmed covertly via the undercover operation."[^cite_note-521] Following the report, Reform UK dropped its support for three candidates because of past racist comments,[^cite_note-lbc-520] and on 30 June, one candidate defected to the Conservatives over a perceived lack of leadership from Reform on the issue.[^cite_note-522][^cite_note-523]

Less than 20% of Reform candidates were women and the five elected were all men and had a median age of 60.[^cite_note-Barker-Singh-524]

#### Results

The party won five seats ([Ashfield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashfield_\(constituency\)), [Clacton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clacton_\(constituency\)), [Boston and Skegness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boston_and_Skegness), [Great Yarmouth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Yarmouth_\(constituency\)), and [South Basildon and East Thurrock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Basildon_and_East_Thurrock)) and came second in 98.[^cite_note-525] Reform's presence split the right-wing vote, allowing Labour to win seats on small margins including [South West Norfolk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_West_Norfolk), [Poole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poole_\(UK_Parliament_constituency\)), [South Dorset](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Dorset) and [Rother Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rother_Valley_\(UK_Parliament_constituency\)). The party won 14% of the vote in total.[^cite_note-meet-62] It became the third-largest party by popular vote, gaining 4,117,610 votes.[^cite_note-526]

Almost 80% of people who voted for Reform had voted for the Conservatives in the previous 2019 election, so 25% of the people who voted for the Conservatives in 2019 switched their vote to Reform in 2024.[^cite_note-527]<sup>: 92–93 </sup>
